After considerable investigation it is now evident
that very little primary information of the type desired
by the Department of Agriculture in this connection can
be expected to become available in Calcutta in the
immediate future. The only subject mentioned in this
request which has prime intorest for commercial ciroles
here is the rice situation in Burma, Thailand and Indo-
esc
China, particularly with respect to possible exports
as soon as Japanese occupation of these territories may
be ended. However, thc firms here formerly engaged
in the rice trade for these territories do not expect
to have any tangible or accurate information until the
military situation develops so as to permit their own
representatives to return to Burma and the other areas
concerned.
With regard to official sources of information
there is also a dearth of primary inf ormation in Calcutt
on this sub ject. It has been suggested by the Foreign
Economic Administration to the American Mission in
New Dalhi that reference should be made to the Far
East Enemy Division of FEA, which has been gathering
information available on this sub ject. The General

=== Page 8 === (confidence 0.93)
POREWORD
This report is intended to present a comprehensive picture of
the wartime food position of Jepan Proper, which may throw some light
on the extent of the food deficiency and may be of guidance in formalating
policies on rationing and on future food production in Japan.
14-03r P9 nobu

=== Page 9 === (confidence 0.98)
-2
SULDARY
Up to 1943 there was no noticeable food shortage in Japan Proper,
tut by the end of that year the food mupply situation became serious
due to lowered domestic prodaction and decreased imports, and declined
still further in 1944 and 1945. Despite strenuous afforts by the
Japanese government in restricting consumption, the carry-over stock
of rice which is the most important staple food reserve, was practically
exhausted by the end of 1943 and nover recovered. Sapply of wheat.
barley and naked barley also decreased by 20 percent in 1945 as compared
with 1941.
Present indications are that the Japanese average daily per capita
food consumption for 1945 * was about 1.809 calories. Compared with
the prewar (1936) daily intake of 2,162 calories, the 1945 level 1s
16 percent lower. This average is estimated primarily on information
supplied by Japanese official sources and on an estimated resident
population of 72,000,000 for 1945. 0f thi daily consumption, 1,622
calories or about 90 percent were derived from domestic production.
Sinco it will be some time before the prewar average can again be assured.
it seems wise to plan 1946 food provisions by adjustments from the 1945
standard.
The tntal food consumed in Jepan in 1945 is estimeted at
21,702.000 m.t. of wh1ch 20.411,000 m.t. were from domestic sources.
* The rice year, starting November 1, of the previous year to October 31.
of the yoar, is used throughout this report for discussions of
consumption, not the calendar year.

from domostie sources, the l946 estimate may he high. Total rice

=== Page 12 === (confidence 0.96)
-7-
The calculation used by the Japanese goverament is based on a
population estimate of 77,133,000. This. however, needs further
scrutiny as to its validity. If we take the total food avallable for
consumption in 1945, 47,514,663 m11lion calories and divide by the 1946
estimated population, the average available for consumption per capita
per day would be 1,688 calories as compared with 1,809 calories in
1945. Purthermore, the 1945 domestic production would only give 1,514
calories per capita per day, calculated on the basis of l946 population
estimate.
Since it will be some time before the Japanese food can gain be
assured at a prewar level of 2,162 in 1936, it 1s better to examine the
effect of 1945 level, 1,809 calories per capita per day on the tealth
and efficiency of the Japanese populace, and make the appropriate
adjustmonts nocessary.
As background, leading up to the present position, there follows
an analysis by commodities of the supply situation during the war years
1941-45.
RICE
I. Supply
Domestic Production - In prewar years, the peak production of rice 1/
was attained in 1939, a total of 68,964,468 koku 2/ (10,085, 000 m.t.).
During the war period, despite offorts of the Japanese government to
increase production of rice, reslts were negative because production

=== Page 13 === (confidence 0.97)

factors such as labor, fertilizers, and implements were limited by the
requirements of war. Farthermore, with the exception of 1942, the rice
crops wers adversely affected by weather conditions. The purchasing
quota systed as well as pric∞ policy implemented by the Japanese govera-
ment had aleo hindered the program of production increase.
Imports - Imports of rice in Jspan depended heavily on the production
in Korea and Fornosa. From the Southern Areas, French Indochima, Burma
and Siam, the Japanese shipping capacity was the determining factor.
Substantial imports of rice were made in 1941 and 1942, but from 1943 on.
imports declined drastically year by year. Only insignificant mounts
were imported in 1945 before the sarrender.
Carry-overs from previous year - The average carry-over for the
ten-year period 1931-40, was 8,721,000 kokm (1,275,000 m.t.) of rice.
This average is roughly about 10 percent of the total rice supply in
Japan for the same period. In 194l-45, the average carry-over was only
3,837.000 kokm (561,000 m.t.) or 44 percent of prewar average stock.
This smounts only to about 5 percent of the total supply in corresponding
years. It is to be noted, however, that according to certain articles
published in Japan 1, the 1944 rice deficit was partly made up by means
of early crops. This would indicate that there was practically no carry-
over from 1944 to 1945. But on tho other hand, the meager amount of
carry-over from 1944 to 1945 as indicated in the present cablegram, 1s in
1 Keizai Mainichi, Tokyo, December 15, 1944.

fact insignificant especially in view of transportation difficulties
that existed im Japam. In other words. for all practicsl purposes.
the Japanese government had exhensted its rice stock every year since
1943.
II. Demand
It has been estimated that an annual export of about 500,000
koku was necessary to meet the demand in Karafuto and possibly some
military outposts outside of Japan. After dedncting exports and
carry-over at the end of each rice year (Oct. 31) from the total supply.
the remaining quantity 1s the disappearance. It is believed that the
Japanese government had made strenuous offorts to out down the waste
in polishing end other non-food uses in order to provide the maximum
quantity for human consumption and seed purposes. The prewar average
(1930-1939) per capita disappearance of rice in Jepsn thus calculated
was 1.081 koku (158 kg.). Assuming that the resident population in
Jepan Proper from 1941 to 1946 renained at 72,000,000 persone 1/ with
out any substantial change, the per cepita disappearance of rice would
amount to 0.981 koku, as the average of the five-year period 1941-45.
It is to be noted that in 1944 and 1945, the average per capita dis-
appearance dropped to 0.947 and 0.847 koku rospectively. Comparing the
1945 figure with the prewer average of 1.081 koku, there is a decrease
of about 22 percent.

amounted to 1,984,000 kolm and 60 percont of which were from Mancharia
It has been reported that the Japanese did get some maize from French
Indo-China in the •arly years of the war perlod 194l-45, but the bulk of
imports is believed to be from Manchuria. It is generally accepted that
maise is used in Japan primarily for industrial purposes and some for
animal feed. Therefore it is not treated as human food in this report.

Daikon - Daikon or giant radish is produced and used artensively
in Japan. It is usually congumed in pickled form. In 1930-36, an
average of 650 million kan was produced (2,438,000 m.t.). s the
production of other vegotables dropped in wartime, it is estimated
that the 1945 production was about 2,000,000 m.t.
Other Tuber and Root Crops - This group includes Taro, Konnyakuimo
(Cuckoo pint), carrots, turnips, burdocks and renkon (root of Indien
lotus). The total average production for 1930-36 was 1.217,000 m.t.
The production for 1945 is estimated at 1,000.000 m.t.
VEGETABLES
Cabbages, cucumbers, egg-plants, mmsk-melons, squash cucumbers,
tometoos, turnips, radishes, watermelons, welsh onions, onions and
16-001 P30 u

=== Page 26 === (confidence 0.98)
-21-
pumpkins are treated here as a whole under the vegetables group. In
1930-36, an annual average of 2,952,000 m.t. was produced. For 1945
it 1s estimated 2,500,000 m.t. were produced.
SUGAR
The averege production of sugar (including cane and beet sugar)
in Japan for 1930-36 was 122,000 m.t., while the 1mports during
1931-37 averaged 906.000 m.t. This gives an average apparent consump-
tion of 1.028,000 m.t. annually. It is estimated that about 150.000
m.t. of sugar was produced in Japan in 1945, and no more than 500,000
m.t. was imported, giving a total of 650,000 m.t. as maximum supply
available for consumption.
FRUITS
It is estimated that there are about 800,000 m.t. of fruits of
all kinds available for consumption in Japan in l945 as compared
with the 1936 figure of 1,300,000 m.t. and represents a decrease of
approximately 40 percent.
KELPS
The production in 1945 of kelps or edible seaweeds is estimated
at approximately 400,000 m.t. Tne averagefor 1931-37 was 539.000
m.t. a year. Hence the 1945 ostimate indicates a decline of about
25 percent.
ANIMAL FOODS
Fiah - Pish is primarily the most important source of animal
14·031 P24 hu

=== Page 27 === (confidence 0.97)
-22
protein in the Japanese diet. The average annual catch in Japan
including coastal and pelagic catches for 1931-37 was 3,352,000 m.t.
In the war period the lack of craft, fuel, and manpover had definitely
ourtailed the annual catch to a great extent. It estimated that the
1945 catch was in the neighborhood of 1,500.000 m.t. or a decline of
about 55 percent.
Aggiculture - In this group are included carp, eel, oyster,
asar1 (Tapes philippinarium) and amanor1 (porphyra). The average
output for 1931-37 was 129.000 m.t. By active encouragement on the
part of the Japanese government, such as fuller utilisation of ponds,
paddy fields, reservoirs, etc., during the wartime, it is estimated
that in 1945 about a total of 150,000 m.t. was produced.
Heat and Poultry. Eees. Milk and Dairy Producta - Due to the
lack of wartime information on these foodstuffs, the following rough
estimates have been made for 1945: 100,000 m.t. of meat and poultry
products, 150.000 m.t. of eggs and about 100,000 m.t. of milk and
dairy products available for consumption in Japan.

FOOD POSITION OF JAPAN PROPER IN 1946
(A Preliminary Report)
SUMMARY
1. The normal inability of Japan Proper to satisfy ite food require-
ments fron domestic production will be seriously aggravated in 1946 accord-
ing to the latest estimatee of the Inperial Japanese Governnent. The pre
sent forecast, as of 9 Novenber 1945, by the Ministry of Agriculture and
Forestry 1s for a total of 27,633,000 etric tons of fcodstuffe available
fron domestic production for consumption in 1946.
2. Thie is almoat 2.000,000 metric tone lower than the total produo-
tion of foodstuffs for 1945. It will provide the average person with only
1325 calories and 40.8 grans cf protein per day as compared with 17e2 cal-
orfee and 58.4 grame of protein fron the 1945 food supply. according to
Japanese officials.
3. Damage fron typhoons and floode during Septenber and October and
unusually cold and wet weather during the latter part of the rice growing
season, are blaned for the dieappointing yields fron current crope. Rice
yields are almost 20 percont below earlier expoctations: sizeable losses
aro also estimated in other grains and in the sweet potato crop.
4. Inequitable food distribution between the rural and urban pop-
ulation, and the diversion of foodetuffs into black market channele are
expected to reduce even more the daily diet for large sections of the pop-
ulation. Japancse officials report a widespread reluctance among farmere
to sell foodstuffs to the government. Soricus difficulties are, therefore,
anticipated in the collection of focdetuff quotas.
5. Inports will be neceesary to bring the Japanese diet to minimum
subsistance levele in 1946. An over-ell average daily per capita diet of
1800 celories would require almost 4,000.000 metric tons of food imports
according to Japanese officials. The Japanese Government has officially
requosted 3,679,400 metric tons of f-odatuffs which, when added to the
present estinate of food production, would provide an average diet per
person per day of approximately 1760 calories.
6. At present, urban residents are receiving far less than these pro-
posed average daily diet levels. The current basic ratin is approximately
1000 calories per person per day. This may be supplenented by non-rationed
foodetuffs in varying amounts depending on their availability. Recent in-
vestigations indicate that in Tokyo the average daily per capita diet, in-
cluding both rationed and non-rationed foode. 1s now approximately 1270
calories and 32 grams of protain.

.Introduction.

ing population, inflation, and the prospect cf serious maldistribution
threaten Japan with a cr1tical food ahcrtage in 1946. The present fore

1e that the total indigenous food production in 19+6 will be alnost

only 1325 calories and t0.8 grans of protein according to Japanese offi-
cials. Present indications are that the daily food intake per pereon in
urbar areas is already below this figure.

appears to be dependert upon the voluce of food imports which may be ex-
ported and the ability of the Japanese Government to solve the problen
of maldistribution.

D. Probleme of Food Distribution in Japan in 1946.
1. The estimeted figure of 1325 crlorics per person per day from
donestic production of foodstuffs in 1946 1o an over-all everage for the
entire Japanese population. The present food purchase program of the Jap-
anese Governnent would leave sufficient food for the agr rian population
to provide an average daily per capita consunption of 18l5 calories; this
leaves only l035 calories for the non-agr:rian population.
a. Indications are, at present, that Japanese Government
will have difficulty n bringing sufficient foodstuffs into the cities to
meet even this diet level. Collections cf rice fron the current harvest
on 20 November 1945 wre only approximately 300,000 metric tons as com-
pared with the veregc collection fron 1942 to 1945 on 20 November of ap-
proxicately 1,030,000tric tons.
2. Officials of the Ministry of Agriculture and Forectry and
independent Japanese agricultural experts attribute the current shortege
of collections to several causes. The l945 rice crop is now estimat-d
at less than 75 percent of the h:rvest of last ye r. Unfavorable weather
has delayed the present rice harvest fron two wesks to a month. Because
of this delay, the rice harvest has extended into the season for planting
wheat. Farmers are seid to heve insufficient time at present to deliver
their rice as rapidly as poesible, All observers agree, however, that
furners are reluctrnt to deliver their assigned food quotes to the govern-
ment and that this constitutes the mejor obstacle in achieving the maxiiun
possible distribution of foodstuffs available fron domestic sources in l946.
3. The prospect of serious difficulties in the coll ction of
rice and other foodstuff quotes fron farners is attributed to ceuses which
are both economic and psychological.
e. Because of the poor rice crop. f: rmers h ve been author-
ized to retain only 80 percent as much rice this year as last year.

This is despite the fact that the governnent has reduced the proportion
of the crop 1t will purchase fron 66 percent in 1945 to 62 percent in 1946.
The present quota progran would leave only sufficient foodetuffs for the
farnere to provide an average daily diet per farn person of l8l5 caloriee.
For rany farmers, this would mean a reduction in their own food supply to
less than sinurun requirenente. Japanese officials adsit that it will be
difficult to induce farners to curtail their consumption below an average
of 2200 calories per farn person per day. If this is true, only about 800
calories will renain as the daily share of the average non-agrarian person.
b. The black narket is a constant and difficult competitor of
the governuent in tho purchase of foodstuffs. This is particularly true
when farmers see an opportunity to barter their crops for necessary physi-
cal conlodities. Agricultural obeervers and officiale believe, however.
that this is not a serious drain on domestic food supplies except in the
environs of the large cities. They point out that transportation facilities
are unavailable for illegal shipnents in larga quantities over a distance.
The proportion of the rice crop which will go into the black warket is dif-
ficult to estinate. Officials of the Ministry of Agriculture and Forestry
predict it will b: less than five percont. Dr. Shiroshi Nasu, Frofensor of
Agricultural Economics at Tokyo Inperial University, believes that black
market sales, in the usual sense, will ba much less than 10 percent of the
conmercial rice supply. He feels, however, that an inpertant notive of the
faruers in withholding rice fron the market is their intention to pay many
of their future operating and living expenses in foodstuffs rather than in
noney. Because of inflation, such "barter" paynents are now encouraged by
those who sell goods and services to the faruers according to Dr. Nacu.
The recent increase in the purchase price of rice fron 92.50 yen to 150 yen
is reportedly considered inadequate by a large portion of farmere. In gen-
eral, however, it is agreed that the ability to obtain farm supplies and
consuner goods is a more important coneideration to the farmer than the
money price he receives for his crops.
c. A general attitude of resentment and hostility toward the
governnent and governnental control is reported to exist anong the rural
population. This is attributed to (1) a reaction fron the rigid control
naintained over agriculture during the war, (2) disillusionment when the
war erded in defeat inetead of pronised victory from which the farhers were
encouraged to expect crent berefits in exchange for their wartile sacri-
fices, and (3) past emyty pronises by the government to supply farners
with fertilizer, other farn supplics, and consumer goode, and to allow
farnere to re-buy foodatuffs at a later date in cases where quotas were
net at the expense of the grower's own minimun food requirenente.
d. Dissatisfaction is reported azong farnere with the pol-
icies and leaderships of the rural agricultural associations (Nogyokai)

which now act as the staple food collection agencies. In many cases under
present leadership, these associations are said to be regarded as organs
for the maintainance of strict governnenial control rather than as bodies
representative of farmers and farn interests. Under prosent law, approval
of leaders by the Prefectural Governor is required and in a great many cases
this has been tantamount to appointment by the Governor.* Present leaders
received such "appointment" during the war because of their ability to
expedite the government war progran rather than because they had the sup-
port and cooperation of the farners. fien they repregent landlord rather
than grower intcrests, and arouse the same resentments fron the farmers
as other agents of centralized control.

foodstuffs produced donestically in 1946, will leave Japan far short of
its minimum food requirenents.

Laboratory. As shown in Natural Resources Section eport No. 3, Food

ceived during the war, and cannot be expeeted under the emergency con-

F. Discussion and Conclusions.
1. The conbination of poor harvests, an incrcase in population,
and a prospect of serious maldistribution, thrcaten Japan Proper with a
serious food shortage during the comins year. Physicel food shortages
within the country, arc not expected by  apanese officials until about
April ls4c, with hpril, May, and June as the most critical months.
2. Maldistribution, however, may leave large sectors of the
population without adequate food long before this tine. Because of de-
clinin stocks of foodstuffs, the averagc deily per capita diet in urban
centers hes alreedy fallen below edequate nutritional requirements. This
present shortege of fond supplies in veri us citics mey be attributed to
the delay in the rice harvest, and may be expocted to improve as rice de
liverics increase.
3. The duration and extent of this improvonent. however. is
dependent on the collection of esteblished rice quotas from farmers.
Japanese officials adnit that full collection of thesc assigned quotes
prescnts serious difficulties because of farmer resistence and because
of diversion of foods into black mark ts. Recontly, meabers of the Min-
istry of Agriculturc end For stry, pcrsonelly appcaled to farners in
verious mural arces to coopcreto in thc ricc collcction progrem. It is
belicved, however, thet edditionel meesures by the governnent will bo
neccssary if Japan is to utilizc its available food supply to achieve the
maximum distribution possible.
4. The conclusion that with
completely equitable distri-
bution, the domestic production of foodstuffs will providc only 1325
celorics per person per day, is besed on en cstimeted population by June
1 46 of 77,133, . persons. This represents en incrcese of almost
2,). ,. persons over the 1 45 population. "hether the actual increase
will be this ler c is subject to qucstion. A populetion of less than
the Jepanese csticate for l946 will. of course, increase the average dai-
ly amont of food aveilakle por person.
5. Finelly it must be noted that all figures on foodatuffs
production arc estimat s made by the  apanese Ministry of agriculture
and Forestry. Physical harvests of individual crops have in no case
been completed. Furthernore, it has not been possible to confirm the
crop reports on which the estimates of the apancse Government are based;
nor hes there been en indopendent audit of the data prosented by the
Japancso Governmont.

Dear Mr. Vincent:
My resignation as an Intelligence Officer with the
Foreign Economic Administration, latterly absorbed by the
State Department, took effect on December 25.
Inasmuch as there are still certain subjects and
incidents arising in connection with myCentral China,
894.
North China, and Japen missions still unreported I expect
to forwardthem to you as fast as they can be formulated.
Enclosed is a self-explanetory report which, in
part, covers mystop-over in Japan from December 9 to De-
cember 19.
Immediately after the trip by jeep from Kyoto to
Omi Hachiman,Major Lardner W. Moore,C.0., 162nd Language

and conclusions to ColonelF. W. Hein, G-2, 6th Army, Kyoto.
Later, from my notes, I went over the whole subject

Section, GHQ, Tokyo, and with Mr. Richard A. May, Chief of
Imports and Exports Division, of the same Section, and as
well with Colenel Munson, G-2, HQ, Tokyo. Therefore,
there has been no actual delay in making a statementof
the case to the authorities prepared and equipped to act.

MR.CLAXTON:
Colonel Gay, of the Civil Affairs Division, dictated
the following to me over the 'phone.He asks if his office
could know this afternoon. (War Dept. 86 - Ext. 4361).
The War Department has received an urgent cable from
General MacArthurstatingthatifhis food requirements can
not be met, he anticipates widespread starvation in the urban
areas of Japan after May lst. His cable requests that im-
mediate actionbe taken to obtain the necessarysupplies to
A
carry outhis directives, or, that this problembe pre-
sented to the Presidentpersonally to clearly define the
9
share of responsibility in the event that a failure occurs.
This has come to the attention of the Secretary of
War who intends to take this matter up immediately with the
President if this matter cannot be decided by Monday night.
A meetinghas, therefore, been arranged with Mr.
Snyder or his representative, Mr. Peterson and General Hill-
0
dring of the War Department and Dr. Fitzgerald of Agriculture
to discuss this matter and determine the intent of the Govern-
ment in supporting the requests made byGeneral MacArthur for
8
food stuffs, for civilian feeding in Japan.
This meeting is tentatively set up to be held in the
Conference Room of the EastWingof the White House at 4:30
on Monday afternoon and your presence is desired at this
meeting.
1

available for this meetingor whether the meeting will be

of the special food mission from Washington, on the basis of hie
studies of the food situation throughout Japan. His formal report
to the Department will, of course, be far more detailed and will
be submitted perhaps before this despatch is received. It is be
lieved pertinent, however, to emphasize the serious political im-
plications of the food crisis in Japan. In full cognisance of the
world-wide shortage of food and of the inappropriateness of an
attempt by an officer with restricted knowledge in the field to
assess relative merit of food needs, no recommendations are offered.
Uqru
A famine in Japan of two or more months duration would in all
probability result in widespread civil disturbance; would soriously
War
retard achievement of the purposesof the occupation; would accel-
Nay
erate and expand movements to destroy capitaliam and to embrace
communiam as a nostrum to solve political, economic, end social
difficulties; and, with probable failure of Government, might do
FEC
irreparable harm to the prestige of a democratic form as contrasted
with totalitarian forms of control of society. Famine or threat of
famine has been the one condition in Japan which has inevitably
brought about disorder and flagrant disregard of authority.
In assessing the significance of the food crisis in Japan, due
consideration should be given to these political factors.
CS/A
Respectfully yours,
WaennRisho
Max W. Bishop
Foreign Service Officer

=== Page 74 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclemure to despatch no. 337 of March 28, 1946, frem the Offioe
of the United States Political Adviser, Tokyo, on the subject
"Food Crisis in Japan".
MEMORANDUM FOR MR. BISHOP
The food supply of the urban Japanese population and the amall
farmers (those with one-fourth acre or less) is expected to become
critical during the first part of May and the crisis will probably
last until at least the middle of July. By July the harvests of
wheat and barley should permit material improvement in distribution
from indigenous supplies, but if the position has deteriorated badly
it is doubtful whether the Government will be able to achieve its
goals for the collection and controlled distribution of these rains.
To date, there has been no exceptional malnutrition and no mjor
difficulty in maintaining the ration of staple grains (except in
Hokkaido). The staple ration, which provides l043 calories daily fer
the normal consumer out of a total intake of l087 calories from the
standard ration, has been supplemented to a minor extent by special
rations and has been widely supplemented by other purchases of un-
rationed food and from the black market. Overall, it is estimated
that the average Japanese non-self-supplier has received about 1700
calories daily during the last six months, with considerable varia-
tion over time and inequitable and inefficient variation among
income classes.
Japanese farmers cultivating more than one-fourth acre are
assumed to be self-sufficient and are not provided for in the ration
plan. Smaller farmers go on the ration when their supplies are ex-
hausted. The self-suppliers are estimated to consume at about 2200-
2300 calories par day, and all experience suggests that it is not
possitle to depress farmers' consumption far below am accustomed level.
Upon these assumptions, founded on fact and confirmed by exper-
ienced judgment, it is estimated by SCAP that there will be available
indigenously for non-self-suppliers only 47l calories daily for the
period (roughly) May-September. This would not be evenly distrib-
uted in time, May and June being the low months, July-September
having increasing supplies. It is possible to assume statistically
greater supplies for July-September than estimated by SCaP. It is
also possible by assuming a lower average population for both halves
of the year, and by assuming a l0% larger rice crop last fal1 than
officially estimated (all reasonable assumptions) to caculate that
about ll00 calories of all foods may be statistically "available"
for consumption by the rationed population during the late spring
and summer. For comparison, it is submitted that the average Japanese
adult requires l420 calories daily for subsistence without any activity.
The operating problem, however, is one of mobilizing supplies
for controlled distribution to almost 60 million persons soon to be
on the ration. Unless all Japanese statistics are to be rejocted
out of hand, present controlled stocks will last on the average about
60 days at the present rate of ration. (Imports now scheduled would
add about one week's supplys Future rice collectiens from farmers
are not

=== Page 75 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
are not expected to be material. Farmers will eat, they have little
faith in either the Government or the yen, and for these reasons as
well as the known insufficiency of fertilimer for next year's crep
it is probable that significant quantities of the "available" rioo
will not be eaten even by farmers this year but will be hearded
straight through nert year's harvest.
The sixty or seventy day controlled stock is, of course, not
evenly distributed. The Government states that it has already en-
countered resistance to movements of stocks from areas of relative
surplus and predicts the use of outright force to prevent movements
in the future. Its reassurances to the local authoritiss are value-
less and would not be believed even if well-founded. Hence widespsead
difficulty in managing the ration may be expected in another month.
The problems may then be expected to cumulate in intensity and to
have serious consequences for the Government's future grain colle
tion program.
Under these circumstances, the wealthy and the tougher poor will
survive. Farmers will engage in some sharing with city relatives.
Even the small remainder of the supply, that controlled by the Gov-
ernment, cannot be expected to be evenly shared. It will probably
be found desirable and necessary to feed the children and also the
werkers in these industries or services that are vital to the occu-
pying forces. Regardless of average statistical availability, these
not falling in one of the above classes will have little or no food
for two months or more in the absence of substantial imports.
It has been suggested that in spite of the preseing world short-
age of grain it would be valuable though expensive insurance proteo-
tion not only against immediate grave problems but against the
prolongation and complication of many of the problems of the occupa-
tion.
Charles J. Shohan
Jarch 27, 1946.

CIVILIAN SUPPLY OF FOOD AND CIVILIAN RELIEF IN JAPAN
1. The Far Eastern Conmission hereby confirms general
policies with respect to the civilian supply of food and
civilian relief in Japan adopted by the U.S. Government in
the fall of 1945 and transmitted to the Supreme Commander
for the Allied Powers to be in effect so long as the present
acute world food shortage exists. The substance of these
policies is as follows:
a. The objectives of the relief policy of the United
States are to prevent such starvation and widespread disease
and civil unrest as would (1) clearly endanger the occupying
forces, and (2) permanently obstruct the ultimate objectives
of the occupation. (Swicc 107, par. 4a)
b. You will not assume any responsibility for the
economic Fehabilitation of Jaran or the strengthening of the
Japanese economy. Ycu will make It clear to the Japanese
people tnat:
a. You assume no obligations to maintain, or
to have maintained, any particular standard of living in
Japan, and
b. That the standard of living will depend
upon the thoroughness with wh! ch Japan riis itself of all
militaristic ambitions, redirects the use of its human and
natural resources wholly and solely for purposes of peaceful
living, administers adequate economic and financial controls,
and cooperates with the occupyin forces and the governments
they represent. (SWic0 52, par. 13)
c. The Japanese authorities will be expected to
develop and effectively carry out programs of working activity
that will enable them out of thelr owm resourees and labor to
accomplish the following:
a. To avoid acute economie distress.
b. To assume just and impartial distribu-
tion of available supplies.
In order to achieve these aims, the Jupanese
authorities will have to make the utmost effort to maximize
produetion of arricultural and fishery products, coal,
charcoal, housine repair materials, clothing and other
essentials. In the event that they fail to do so, you will
direct them to take such measures as in your judgment are
necessary. (SWN00 52, par. 19)

=== Page 85 === (confidence 0.95)
-2 -
d. You will assure that all practicable economic and
police measures are teken to achieve the maximum utilization
of essential Japan ese resources in order that Imports into
Japan may be strictly limited. Such measures wiil include
production and price controls, ratloning, control of black
markets, fiscal and financial controls and other measures
iirected toward full emplopment of resources, facilities and
means available 1n Jaran. (SW:30 52, par. 29a)
e. You viil be responsible for providing imported
surplies only to supplement iocal rescurces and only to the
extent supplementation is needed to prevent such widespread
disease or civil unress us would endanrer the occupying forces.
Such imports will be confine! to minimum quantities of foods,
fuel, medical and sanitary supplies and other essential items,
including t ose wh:ch will enable local production of such
supplies whi ch you woult ot erwi se have to import. (swicc 52,
par.291)
f. Iroducticn of food crops should be maximized.
(swicc 98, par. 4b) Supplies of fertilizers should be made
available. (Swict 98, par. 4c) The production of farm tools
and implements in short supply should be encouraged. (SWNCC
99, par. 4d) Special uttentton should be iven to encourage
procuction und sule of Incentive oods to induce farmers
and other rood producers te move their produce to market.
(SWnce 107, par. 4h) The ex!stin- dispersion of persons from
food deficit areas to food surplus areas should be prolonged,
exceptions bein: mude for people of needed special skills.
(SWNC0 107, par 4g)
2. The Far ast Commission recommends to its member
rovernments that us a matter of policy they take no step
which would have the effeet of providing imports of food
to the Japanese wil ch would represent priority or preferen-
tial treatment for the fapanese over the requirements of the
peoples of any Allied power or literated area, except to the
extent that the Supreme Cormanier for the Allied Powers has
determined that imnorts :nto Japan are essential immediately
for the safety of the occupatien forces. Th's policy shall
remain in effect durine any period of acute world food
shortae.

SUBJECT:
Food Policy Paper
1. Twenty-nine days ago today, General Hilldring, with con-
siderable difficulty and embarrassment, persuaded the FEC to
refer back to Committee No. 2 the question of a food and civilian
relief policy for Japan. It was understood by the Commission that
this step, representing the last in a long series of embarrassing
incidents in the handling of this subject by the U.S., was de-
signed to permit the U.S. to submit promptly a new statement
which it could fully support clear through to final FEC action.
2. Twenty-eight days ago today, JK submitted to General Hilldring
a proposed new statement to which he gave his general approval,
subject orly to a request that it be shortened.
3. As of today the U.S. Delegation on the FEC has not received
any instructions with respect to the item of food and civilian
relief policy which is on the Committee No. 2 agenda at the request
of General Hilldring.

=== Page 99 === (confidence 0.99)
Statement by U. S. Relative to FEC Policy on Food
The U. S. approves the policy statement on food con-
tained in FEC
on the understanding that:
1. As a first step in implementation of this
policy the Supreme Commander will present his require-
ments for food imports to the U. S. Government as re-
quested by current directives classified separately as
to:
a. Requirements essential to the safety of
the occupation forces.
b. Additlonal requirements necessary to
meet the objectives of the occupation.
2. In order to make it possible for the U. S. Govern-
ment to "take all steps within its power to assist this
(FEC food) policy being carried out" SCAP will forward to
the U. S. Government all necessary data as to Japanese
indi enous resources, measures being taken to increase
production and provide equitable distribution of such
resources, dietary standards necessary to prevent disease
and unrest such as would endanger the safety of the
occupation forces and other required information.
3. The U. S. Covernment will determine, on the
basis of information submitted from SCAP and other in-
formation availeble to it through consultation with other
international bodies or otherwise, the imports other
than those needed for the safety of the occupation forces
which would not have the effect of giving priority or
preferentiel treatment to the Japanese.
4. The U. S. Government will then take steps to
assist the implementation of the Commission policy and
will inform the Supreme Commander of its views as to the
maximum imports which would not provide priority or
preferential treatment for the Japanese.

=== Page 103 === (confidence 0.94)
21
3
Ref. 1706/-/46
AIPE 
The Uni ted Kingdom Government are much perturbed
894.5018/12-2046
by the information which reaches them as to the present
policy of food collection and distribution in Japan. Stated
in general terms, their anxiety arises in the first place
from the fact that the per capita food supply now available
to the Japanese people is much greater than that available
to the people of territories in South East Asia for which
the United Kingdom is responsible, a disparity concerning
61317
which they are liable to attack in Parliament and in the
Press. They are further influenced by the striking anomaly
of a si tuation in which the Japanese people are so fortunate
as to be receiving increased rations, which put them in a
better position than during the war, whereas the people of
the United Kingdom are in a worse position and even faced
wi th the prospect of further reductions.
There are three main points to which His Majesty's
Government attach importance:
1.
The rice collection, its relative proportion to
S
the crop, and the measures taken by the Japanese
to see that the quota is in fact collected.
V
2.
The consumption level, not only of the staple
the inadequate measures which the Japanese have
taken for the equalization of consumption.
)
3./
44

=== Page 104 === (confidence 0.98)
2
2.
3.
The attitude of the Japanese Government to their
own responsibilities, and the extent
to which the
occupying authori ty should endeavour to regulate
that attitude.
Taking these points separately, the views of
His Majesty's Government, stated on broad lines and with
a minimum of statistical elaboration, are as follows:
1.
Rice Collection. Out of a total crop for the
1947 crop year estimated at 63 million koku or 9 million
tons, the Japanese Government proposes to collect 28 million
koku (4 million tons). This i. an even smaller proportion
of the total than the target figure of last year. It
leaves 35 million koku or 5 million tons to be consumed
by the self-supplying part of the population, who account
for only 43% of the total population.
It is admittedly difficult to exercise full
control over consumption by self-suppliers, but unless the
Japanese government can ¿et more than 4,000,000 tons of
this year's rice crop into its hands, it is to be feared
that over-consumption by self-suppliers and the black
market will in 1947 again jeopardize the staple ration
for urban Japan.
It would be unrealistic to suggest that the self-
suppliers should be reduced to the st andard of the non-self
suppliers; but a rough estimate shows that, if methods of
collection were improved, for every lo0 calories per diem
taken from self-suppliers, imports of food could be
reduced by 380,000 tons per annum.
These/

=== Page 105 === (confidence 0.97)
C3
3.
These ana related facts constitute, in the view of
the United Kingdom Government, most urgent reasons for
strengthening and improving the control and di stribution of
rice ana other indigenous foodstuffs in Japan. On present
estimates, the self-suppliers, - less than half the popula-
tion - will consume some 70% of all indigenous supplies in
1947.
2.
The consumption level. Before the war rice con-
sumption per capita per diem in Japan was about 13 oz.
The current ration is 12.8 oz. which 1s well above the
war-time ration of 10.24 oz.
The Japanese have asked for imports of 2 million
tons or 14 million koku of rice equivalents, which would give
them a total of 77 million koku of rice or imported rice
equivalents. That is to say, the Japanese are aiming at
1 koku (of rice and imported foodstuffs in rice equivalent)
per head of the population per annum, which is the average
pre-war consumption, and this takes no account of indigenous
supplies of other foods.
The figures cited above are based upon information
which appears to be reliable, but His Ma jesty's Government
have not seen any detailed official estimates by the
Japanese Government of indigenous resources and proposed
consumption in 1947. They assume that the S.C.A. P. obtained
such an estimate before sanctioning the increased ration,
and would be grateful if, as in 1946, it could be furnished
to them.
3.1

=== Page 106 === (confidence 0.96)
2
1
40
3.
The Attitude of the Japanese Government.
It is
no wonder that the Japanese daily press and such organs as
the Oriental Economist write optimistically of the food
situation, refer to the "unbelievable generosity" of the
occupying authority and by implication approve t he imper-
fection an corruption in food distrihution.
The Japanese Government for its part appears to
be taking it for granted that une qual consumption levels,
black markets, and other shortcomings in food administra-
tion are inevitable and incurable; and there are not
wanting, in intelligence reports from the Supreme Commander's
Headquarters, indications that some officials there take a
similar pessimistic view.
The United Kingdom Government therefore feel
justified in enguirin whether the Supreme Comander is
definitely of the opinion that no further pressure can be
brought to bear upon the Japanese Government to induce
them to improve their controls and bring about more
equitable distribution, by declaring that imports of food
will be conditional upon such action or by other appropriate
means.

MEMORANDUM FOR FILES
January 17, 1947
FW
894.5018/12-2046
The attached Memorandum was handed to General Hilldring
as an informal record of a discussion which took place among
Sir George Sansom, U.K. representative of the Far-Eastern Com-
mission, General Hilldring, Ross Whitman and me.
Sir George pointed out that the U.K. was gravely concerned
over the present rice collection, level of consumption, and the
attitude of the Japanese Government concerning food distribution
problems. He also registered a complaint concerning the lack
of adequate information from SCAP on the intermal problems of
Japan with which members of FEC are concerned.
General Hilldring pointed out to Sir George that we in the
State Department and also officials of the War Department are
directly concerned with the seme problems end had, therefore,
planned to send a mission, consisting of representatives from
the Agriculture, War and State Departments, to look into all
phases of the food problems in Japan. It was further pointed
out that it is entirely possible that this mission may find it
necessary to recommend a reduction of import requirements and an
increase in the present collection of indigenous production.
Ceneral Hilldring assured Sir George that they will be kept
fully informed of the progress of the mission.

CONFIDENTIAL
Office Memorandum'
A-H
TO
:A-H - Mr. Gross-
DATE: March 5, 1947
FROM :JK - Mr. MaPtAn
SUBJECT:Letter of Transmittal to U.S. Member, FEC, Accompanying SNCC 331/3
894.5018/3-547
Regarding Food Policy for Japan
1. It is recommended that the letter of transmittal
accompanying SWNCC 331/3 be forwarded to the U.S. Member of FEC
without modification.
2. The U.S. Member of FEC is authorized to approve amend-
ments in wording of U.S. policy statements which do not change their
substance. It is believed that the amendment below to paragraph 2
of SyNCC 331/3 in no way compromises the substance of U.S. policy
regarding food for Japan and may conciliate other members of the FEC
who express resentment over U.S. deletion of a clause (lb(l)) which
they consider to express no more than a matter of fact.
2. The Far Eastern Commission decides as a matter of
policy that during any period of acute world food shortage,
no imports of food should be permitted which would have the
effect of giving priority or preferential treatment for the
Japanese over the requirements of the peoples of any Allied
Power or liverated area, many of which suffer shortages of
food largely due to Japanese aggression, except to the extent
that the Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers has
CS/V
determined that imports into Japan are essential for the
safety of the occupation forces.
3. It is recommended that the U.S. Member of FEC be
authorized to approve in the Far Eastern Commission the amendment
to paragraph 2 indicated above.
4. Colonel Fahey of the War Department believes that he
894.5018
U.S. member of FEC should resist any pressure to bring about changes
TLED
in SWNCC 331/3 even if those changes be merely verbal in charactor.
However, he admits that the suggested amendment to paragraph 2 or
SINCC 331/3 is entirely without substance and that it would be
within the authority of the U.S. Member of FEC to agree to it.
47
Smai
DCH F U.
v2
3
547
JA

D. The Legel Basis for Compulsory Foodstuffe Collections
1. Dolivery of foodstuffc to the governnent by individual far-
mers up to qucta levels is required by law. In case of failure to fulfil
quntes, two types of lcgal neasures may be initiated to compel doliveries.
2. The first type of legal ection leads to penelties of imprison-
ment or fines. Under the provieinne of the Fondstuffs Control Law of 21

Februery 1942 (as anenied by Law No 53, March 1943, and Law No 4, Fobruary

otherwise virlate crlinances rclatine to the flow of foodstuffs through
official channels are subject to fines up to ¥50,000 or to confinement at
hard labor for a period not to exceed ten years. In practice, this type of
prosecuticn is iritiated by the prefectural procurator, who prefers charges

SUBJECT: Food policy for Japan
Attached is a copy of a paper on the food policy
which the UK Delegate is to place before the Commission.
I discussed this with Phil Claxton and he said you would
take it up with the War Department. If you wish to re
fresh yourself on the latest phase of the controversy
refer to the minutes of the Commission in late February.
You will note that the UK paper is much simpler
than FECm026/12, that 1t concedes the U. S. position on
8
no publicity for Japanese relative food status, but that
it also eliminates explicit reference to the fact that
SCAP and the U. S. are already acting under the same
policy. This might be all right, however, in view of
the fact that the UK draft eliminates most of the material
C
in FEC-026/12 which had been taken from previous directives.

SUPPLY OF FOOD FOR CIVILIAN RELIEF IN JAPAN
1. The Far Bastern Commission, having considered
the question of the supply of food for civilian relief
in Japan in the light of
(a) the measures already taken by the Supreme
Commander since the beginning of the occupation to
improve Japan's indigenous food resources, and
(b) the acute shortage of food which is not con-
fined to Japan, but is causing serious hardship in
countries which suffered as a result of Japanese aggression;
hereby adopts the following policies with respect to this
matter:
2. The Supreme Commander should ensure by all
practicable means, that the Japanese Government take the
necessary measures
(a) to attain the maximum production of indigenous
food; and
(b) the ensure equitable distribution of indigenous
food supplies by maintaining and improving the system of
collection, rationing and price control.
3. During the period of acute world shortage imports
of food for Japan should be the minimum required to pre-
vent such starvation and widespread disease and civil un-
rest as would endanger the safety of the occupation forces,
and no imports exceeding this minimum should be permitted
which

=== Page 253 === (confidence 0.96)

which would have the effect of giving preferentiel
treatment to the Japanese over the peoples of any Allied
Power or liberated area.
4. The Far Eastern Commi ssion recommends to 1ts
member governments that they take all steps within their
power to assist the implementation of this policy.

=== Page 261 === (confidence 0.99)

WAR DEPARTMENT
WASHINGTON
September 17, 1947
The Honorable
The Secretary of Agriculture
Washington, D. C.
Dear Mr. Secretary:
The State and War Departments are gravely concerned as
to the food situation in Japan and Korea. The world shortage
of grains will have a particularly unsettling effect in Japan,
unless wheat deliveries are maintained at a minimum level of
1,700,000 metr1c tons, and for Korea 400,000 metr1c tons, (both
total product weight) during the fiscal year 1948.
These quantities are essential minima to the maintenance
of a calorie ration of 1246 in Japan and 1550 in Korea. We feel
that the 1246 calorie ration for the 78,000,000 people of Japan
is a bare existence diet, below which disease and unrest would
inevitably follow.
In addition to the above indicated grain levels, the
War Department must exploit every opportunity of obtaining
supplemental foods in order that the ration may be attained.
In view of the fact that the maintenance of life, law,
and order in Japan and Korea is a commitment of the United States
under international law, as well as from a purely humanitarian
point of view, the above request is submitted in order to enable
the War Department to implement the United States national policy
as it affects these two countries.
Sincerely yours,
Secretary of war
Under Secretary of State

M. SALTZMAN
Septembor 19, 1947
OE - Mr. Whitman
Food requirements for Korea, Japan and Ryukyus - fiscal
year 1948.
I have revlewed a number of estimates of food re-
quirements for these areas in fiscal year 1948. The
requd rements vary considerably, ranging from a total
I Fc estimate of 1,900,000 tons for all three areas, to
an estimate for Japan alone of 1,940,000 tons of wheat
equivalent, the latest SCAP f1gure. 1,700,000 tons
wheat equivalent is the latest War Department estimate.
To the latter two figures must be added an estimated
80,000 tons for the Ryukyus and approx1mately 400,000
tons for Korea, or an over-all range of 1,900,000 to
2,400,000 tons. All estimates are based on the assumed
1dentical feeding level of the 1,246 ration for the
normel consumer in Japan, with the feeding level of ap-
proximately 1,550 calories; a cereal ration of 1,231
calories in Korea but with a feeding level higher than
in Japan. The maintenance of the ration in Japan and
Korea 1s essential to the achievement of U. S. objectives
in this area, looking toward self-support of the economies
and the creation of a favorable political situation.
The higher estimates for Japan given by the War
Department and SCAi provide an allowance for serious crop
damage from August floods in the amount of 375,000 tons
but do not allow for poss1bly 200,000 ton loss due to the
recent typhoon. Whether there ls other justification for
the low IFFc figure is not known but it is clear that the
I FC shipments during fiscal year l947 failed to meet the
ration requirements of the Japanese by over 350,000 tons.
It is my conclusion that for the purposes of esti-
matin; over-all requirements for shipment of U. S. grain,
a figure of 2,400,000 for the three areas should be used.
It Is obvious that the latest War Department figure of
roughly 2,200,000 wheat equivalent is an absolute minimum
in view of recent crop damage.
I do not concur in the proposal to meet only a part
of Japan's requirements while meeting Germany's in full.

=== Page 263 === (confidence 0.98)
ITP - Mr. Nitse
September 19, 1947
OE - Mr. doWilde
Cereal "equirements of the US/Ux Zonss in Germany
The US/UK zones requires about 4,200,000 of cereals in terms of wheat
oquivalent (3,800,000 from the US, and the balance from other sources) in
order to meet the present ration scale which provides 1550 calories per day
for the so-called normal consumer. Such a quantity of cereals will insure the
average non-self-supplier, as distinct from the normal consumer, a food intake
of only 1900 to 2000 calories, and not, as IFI seems to indicate, a consumptiom
of 245 calories. While we cannot assure the US/UK sones in Germany a supply
which would provide a higher food intake than that in liberated countries of
Western Burope, the importance of maintaining a minimum food standard in Ger-
many should be stressed. Ever since the cessation of hostilities, the Garmans
have been consistently among the worst fed people in Europe. While it is omly
just and proper as long as supplies are short that they should be less well fod
than other peoples, they should be provided with a basic minimum. In my opin-
ion an average non-farm consumption of 1900-2000 calories is such a basic mini-
mum and should be maintained barring really dire emergencies. It should be
pointed out that the provision of a basic minimum to the Germans is really in
the interest of Burope. They cannot be expected to mine and transport coal
or produce other goods required for European economic recovery unless they have
enough food to sustain productive work.
To the best of my knowledge the requirements givon above are not over-
stated, although I have had no opportunity to examine them personally. There
have been recent well-subatantiated reports that the food situation in Germany
has further deteriorated in that the potato crop is now expected to be sub-
stantially less than originally anticipated.
It is fully realized that the stated requirements would provide Germany
with more cereals per capita than a number of other European countries. It
should be emphasised, however, that Germans have far less non-cereal foodstuffs
available to them than other Western European countries. Their diet has for
the past few years been inferior to that in Western European countries, not
only in quantity (calories),but also in quality. Potatoes and cereals are vir-
tually the only staples available.

the program at the meeting of the Cabinet Committee on World Fred Progrems

casily available.
ORIERAL RECONDMENDATICIS

organiged and membership broadened. It might be renamed the Emerganey

B.) C.C.C. procurement of grains should be direoted tomrd obtaining
the maximm quantities by Desember 31, 1947. To improve the rate of pro-
curement, giving the use of tax deferral certifieates permitting the
deelaration of income in 1948 should be considered immediately. Buying
methods should also be sxamined and revised if necessary.
C.) Legislation should be obtained at the earliest possible moment
reestablishing allocation controls and obtaining controls over trading
margins.
0.) The Department should aasign one man with responsibility to ex-
pedite and follow throuh cn the program.
The program recommended above is essentially the one followed at the
time of the emergency during 1945-1946. The Government did not meve,
however, wntil late in the period. It succeeded in enabling us to meet
the program. The main difference is the reeommandation to begin positive
action now. With earlier and wore inteksive application, it is felt thet
sucsess can be achieved under the more diffioult conditions now existing.

FAR EASTERN COMMISSION
2516 MASSACHUSETTS AVENUE, N. W.
WASHINGTON 8. D. C.
22 October 1947
Dear Mr. Saltzman:
I should like to write you briefly in regard to the amendment,
proposed by the Department of the Army, to the pending FEC paper, en-
titled "Supply of Food for Civilian Consumption in Japan," with which
I know you are familiar. I am presenting for your consideration my
judgment of the effects which this amendment would have on the Far East-
ern Commission, on the leadership of the United States in that organiza-
tion, and on the principle of international cooperation.
Ir. Whitman has already pointed out in a memorandun to you that
this amondment is unnecessary, since the United States has never sent
to General MacArthur all of the food which he has requested under the
894.
"disease and unrest" formula, and probably will not be able to do so
during the coming year. On the other hand the term "objectives of the
occupation," if adopted as a limitation on the amount of food which the
United States might export to Japan, would constitute practically no
limitation at all since the expression is extremely elastic.
I wish, however, to confine my comment to the probable effect of
the amendment of the Department of the Army on the Far Eastern Commission,
which from its beginning has been under the leadership of the United States,
and on the principle of international cooperation which the United States
Government has repeatedly advocated and supported. The United States
0
-2247
Government, months before the close of the war against Japan, drew up the
Terms of Reference for a Far Eastern Advisory Commission. After the war,
the Far Eastern Advisory Comnission developed under United States leader-
ship into the present Far Eastern Commission.
I have received many letters complimenting the Far Eastern Com-
mission on its record of accomplishment, especially in comparison with
other somewhat similar organizat ons. The Far Eastern Commission has
enabled the United States to obtain the cooperation of the other FEC
countries instead of their possible opposition, and it has in general
iven to the U.S. policies for the occupation of Japan the support of the
other ten countries interested in the Far East.
The amendment of the Army, in my judgment, would tend to impair
the confidence of other Governments in the integrity of the United States,
to antagonize the other members of the Far Lastern Commission, to weaken
The Honorable
Charles E. Saltzman
Assistant Secretary of State

=== Page 275 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2-
international cooperation in the Far East, and to lessen the effectiveness
of the Commission and the leadership of the United States.
1. The amendment would tend to impair the confidence of other
Governments in the integrity of the United States Government.
It would probably have this effect since the U.S. Government has
repeatedly informed the Far Eastern Commission during the past year and a
half that it approves the principle regarding the supply of food for civilian
consumption in apan, which is embodied in the pending food paper.
The United States gave its approval when its representative voted
For the FEC Policy Lecision on Food Supply for Jepan, April 25, 1946. This
policy decision is now binding on the United States as well as on the other
states in the FEC.
It cave its approval arain in November, 1946, when a new food paper
was introduced into the F.C.
It gave its approval finally, in August, 1947, in announcing its
support of the pending paper.
The food paper, now before the FEC, omits all the points to which the
U.S. Goverment previcusly raised objection. If the basic principle of this
food paper should now, after a year and a half's support, be repudiated by
the .S. Government, the effect on the other Governments would be serious.
2. It would tend to antagonize the other states on the FEC.
Cther Fc states, especially those where there is a serious food
shortage, such as India, China, the Philippines, and the United Kingdom on
behalf of its de endencies, have been greatly concermed over the food problem.
The emotions of their representatives have been more deeply stirred by this
issue than by any other before the Far Eastern Commission. They already are
apprehensive that the .S. Goveriment is treating Japan, a former enemy, more
gonerously than it is treating its Allies. They recognize, however, that
SCAP is untitled to ask for food to mect the "disease and unrest" formula,
even though a similar amount of food is not available for Allied territories
in the Far East. Eut if the United States Government should ask for enough
focd for Japan to meet tie elastic formula, "the objectives of the occupa-
tion," they would strongly resent it, and many of them would regard it as
evidence that the United States is attempting to build up Japan at the ex-
pense of the Allies of the United States.
3. It would tend to weaken international cooperation in the Far East.
The shortage of food is a problem conmon to most countries repre-
sented on the Far Eastern Commission. All of them support "the disease
and unrest" formula, including at present the United States. If the U.S.
Government should disregard the known position of all of the other Govern-
ments and urge a policy wihich they strongly disapprove, it would naturally
make then. less inclined to cooperate with the United States on other issues.

=== Page 276 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
4. It would tend to lessen the effectiveness of the Commission
and the leadership of the United States.
The effectiveness of the FEC depends in large measure upon the
number of important policy decisions it is able to adopt. In the FEC, as
in most international conferences, the normal procedure is: disoussion,
compromise, agreement. If the United States refuses to make any compromise
in its position, especially in regard to a paper deemed of great importance
by the other states, they in turn will be less inclined to compromise, and
the FEC will approve fewer policy decisions. The leadership of the United
States, too, depends in some part on its giving due consideration to the
views of its colleagues. In general they have thus far shown a willingness
to recognize the lead of the United States, and in considerable measure to
follow it.
In conclusion, I believe that the presentation to the FEC of the
Army amendment as a new policy of the Lnited States would inflict a needless
blow on the Far Eastern Commission, on the leadership of the United States,
and on the U.S. policy of international cooperation in the Far East.
Yours sincerely,
rank
Weap
Frank R. McCoy

"During the. period of acute world shortages, imports
of food for Japan should be the minimum required to attain
the objectives of the occupation and to prevent such starva-
tion and widespread disease and civil unrest as would endanger
the safety of the occupation forces, and no imports exceeding
this minimum should be pemitted which would have the effect of
giving preferential treatment to the Japanese over the peoples
of any Allied Power or liberated area."
ts: For more tnan a year and a half other members of the FEC have
pressed the United States to egree to a statement of policy regarding
suply of food for civilien consumtion in Japan. The FEC, with
U. 3. concurrence, made a policy decision April 25, 1946 (Appendix B)

that imports ere essential irmediately for the safety of the Occupa-

of giving to the Japenese e priority or preferentiel treatment over
the roquirements of the peonles or any Allied Power or liberated area".
The United Stetes regarded this decision as binding on it but rerused

力
mit a policy paper which might be transmiuted to SCAP. Such a paper
was anproved by SnICC October 31, 1945 in SiCC 331 and introduced

Following discussion in FEc, it wes necessary to modify parts of
the peper, which was done in ncc 331/3 in February 1947 (Appendix C),
which reaffimued the essential provision regarding level of feeding.
The United States reaffirmed its sunport of its provision in FEC. In

for determining the food sunnly to be allowed Jepan, the United States

Discussion: This amendment should not be agreed to by the Department of
State because
A.
It contravenes the long-established US position
taken in FEC.
akove
As stated/in e eats, the U.S. has arproved the princinle objected

B. Substantively it would seriously affect our relations

Other countries, esnecially those with food shortages, have been
Greatly concerned over the food problem. These countries know and largely
accept

=== Page 279 === (confidence 0.99)
-3
accept the fact that, under the disease and unrest formula, the
Japanese are already being fed at a level higher than some of the
people of some of the Allied nations. However, if the U.S. Govern-
ment should now ask for enough food for Japan to meet the elastio
formula "the objectives of the occupation" during a period of acmte
food shortage as a preference of priority over the needs of the neigh-
boring liberated Allied countries, they would strongly resent it and
might regard it as evidence that this Government is attempting to
build up Japan at the expense of the Allies of the United States.
General McCoy, U.S. Member of FEC, has urged most strongly the
points made here in "A" and "B" (Letter at Appendix E).
C. It is entirely unnecessary in protecting the
interests of the United Statea.
SCAP, who, the FEC agrees, will be a United States Officer, is
responsible under the policy statement for estimates of "disease and
unrest" requirements, which are flexible. These requirements have
always been greater than the shipments permitted by the world supply
situation, and will continue to be so in the foreseeable future. The
paper is effective only during the period of acute world food short-
age, and it is entirely within the power of the U.S. to determine
when this condition ends. So long as such condition does in fact
exist, it is inconceivable as a practical matter that the U.S. would
in fact avowedly give Japan preferential treatment over liberated
countries. The provision in the minutes allows the U.S. to determine
what level of supply can be provided without giving preferential treat-
ment. Finelly, the U.S. can protect ite interests in food supply to
Japan through the actions of its representatives in the IEFc where, as
the principal exporting government to Japan, it will be bound only by
allocations its representatives approve.
Note: FEC 248, now under consideration,deleted a number of para-
graphs of SWNCC 33l/3. In authorizing the U.S. member in FEC to pro-
ceed with FEC 248. State, with Department of the Army concurrence,
considered that these deletions were not injurious to U.S. interests.
Army now argues that the deletion of two of them might be injurious.
They are:
1. "The objectives of relief policy are to prevent such
starvation and widespread disease and civil unrest as would (1)
clearly endanger the occupying forces, and (2) permanently ob-
struct the ultimate objectives of the occupation."
2. "The Supreme Commander should be responsible for pro-
viding imported food supplies only to supplement local resources
and only to the extent supplementation is needed to prevent such
widespread disease ar civil unrest as would endanger the occupy-
ing forces or permanently obstruct the ultimate objectives of
the occupation."
These

=== Page 280 === (confidence 0.97)
These statements were taken from earlier directives toSCaP whioh were
intended to restrict SCAP's and U.S. responsibility for imports to the
minimm necessary for Japanese needs. (Note the emphasis on "clearly
endanger" and"permanently obstruct".) Furthermore, paragraphs 2 and
3 of Swecc 331/3, which are in essence the same as FEc 24s, olearly
embraced these provisions end were intended to be the effective language
during acute world food shortage.
D. Insistence on the Army amendment will result in
refusal of the FBC to pess the paper and leave
the U.S. subieet to the FEC polioy of April 25.
1946 (Appendix B).

paper without the Army amendment.

Mr. Jesse Gilmer, Administrator

Department of Agriculture
Washington 25, D. C.
Dear Mr. Gilmer:
Confirming our conversation of 22 October 1947 and
pursuant to Under Secretary Draper's earlier request to Sec-
retary Anderson, we are very greatly concerned abeut the small
current shipments of cereals to Japan and Korea, and earnestly
ask the assistance of the Department of Agriculture in this
matter. I inclose a cable from General MacArthur which speaks
for itself.
He recognize that under existing unsettled conditions
it serves no useful purpose to discuss the present tarcet which
the Department of Agriculture has tentatively fixed for ship-
ment of cereals to the Pacific occupied areas during the fiscal
year 1948. We do believe, however, that it is desirable from
every viewpoint, yours as well as ours, to ship as large a part
of this total target at as early & date as possible. We ask
that this be done on the underatanding that we will inform the
theater commends in Japan end Koree that such amounts are mere-
ly pre-shipments against the tar et of which they have already
been informed, and that there is no a surance, therefore, that
these shipments will increase the total amounts of coreal im-
ports from the United States during the fiscal year; that on
this hasis the supplies so pre-shi ped should be conserved and
that the ration not be incrensed based upon such pre-shipments.
Further, we unders and that such pre-shi ments would
be made without any change in sllocations and merely as advance
shipments against prospective future allocations.
e urge that there are grest advantages in this course:
1. It would msintain a more regular flow of supply
which would enable us to manage our shipping problems.
Otherwise, ships now on this run will be lost and diffi-
cultier in replacing them at a later time are likely, es-
pecially when the Marshall Plan goes into effect and creates
a laree demand for ships.

=== Page 294 === (confidence 0.99)
CONFIDENTIAL
2. At the present time, there is graim available im the
nerthwest which cam be shipped frem the West Coast at a much
lower shipping cost to us and in shorter time. This will ob-
viate later long and expensive shi ments ef sorrespending
amounts from the Gulf. This is a sounder paliey than to ship
West Coast grain to France as has recently been done at times.
3. This would not increase the total obligation of the
Department of Agriculture later in the year because the supply
so pre-shipped would remain under the control of the Amerioan
military government. There is a great difference between such
shipments to the occupied areas on the one hand and shipments
to other countries. In the latter, control of the use is lost
upon arrival, as a result of which the grain may be eonsused
and ths demand renewed later.
4. The shipments can be made as pre-shipments without
any alteration in the over-all policies as to total monshly
allocation.
5. The shipment of grain at this time will reduee the
danger of e situation occurring next Spring, in whieh famine
conditions in Japan and Korea may create demands fer large
shipments to those areas at & time when the Marshall Plan
countries and other friendly nations are acutely in need of
food and when it would be awkward to inorease the shipments
for our occupied areas. We should not repeat our mistake of
last year in this regard.
6. Such shipmonts will permit a mixing of imported grains
with rice in a way to get the maxiwum utilisation from both as
the anexed cable points out.
Sincerely yours,
(Signed) TRACY S. VOORHEES
Tracy S. Voorhees
Department of the Army Food Administrator
for the Occupied Areas
