WASHINGTON
October 29, 1947
Dear I.r. Draper:
I understand that the Depertment of the Army wishes to have
paragraph 3 of FEC 248/4 - Sunply of Food for Civilian Consumption
in Japan, amended to provide that imports of food for Japan should
be the minimum required to attain the "objectives of the occupa-
tion", as well es prevent disease and civil unrest. The Department
of State feels that the suggested anendment is unacceptable for
the following reesons.
1. The amendment is unnecessery for protection of United
States interests in Janan. This question was considered by the
Denartments over many months and it was agreed thet while the
addition of the "objectives of the occupation" might have some merit,
the dieease and unrest formmula was adequate. S'Ncc confirmed this
position at leest twice in action on the STNCC 33l series. SCAP's
estimates of diseese and unrest requirements are flexible and have
always been greeter than the shipments permitted by the world supply
situation, and will continue to be so in the foreseeable future.
Furthermore, the United States can protect its interests in Japan
through the actions of its representative in the IEFC where, as you
know, exporting governments are bound only by ellocetions their
representatives annrove.
2. The proposed amendment would have serious consequences
for continued internctionel cooneration in the Far Enst. Introduction
of the amenament would tend to impair confidence of other
governments in the United States since the United States has repeatedly
stated it approved the peper with the present wording and is, in
fact, committed to it by FC action on April 25, 1946. Other
countries kmow, and largely accent the fact, that already Japen
is receiving under the disease and unrest formula more food than some
Allied nations. But if the United States Government should now
ask for enough food for Janan to meet the elastic formula, "the
objectives of the occupetion" as a priority over their own needs.
they would strongly resent it end might regard it as evidence that
this Government is attempting to build up Japan at the expense of the
Allies
The Honorable
William H. Draper, Jr.
Under Secretary of the Army,
Denartment of the Army.

=== Page 299 === (confidence 0.99)
2
Allies of the United States. An attempt by the United States to
repudiate its previous commitments and to urge a position which
is known to be strongly disapproved by other governments, would
gravely increase the problem of obtaining their cooperation in
other important matters.
For these reasons it would be appreciated if the Department of
the Army withdrew their proposed amendment to FEC 248/4.
Charles E. Saltzman

=== Page 300 === (confidence 0.97)
A-S
DEPARTMENT OF THE ARMY
NOV?
OFFICE OF THE UNDER SECRETARY
WASHINGTON, D. C.
1V D
NOV 3 - 1947
Honorable Cherles E. Saltzman
Assistant Secretary of State
Department of State
Dear Mr. Saltzman:
The Department of the Army has given careful consideration
to FEC-248/4, dated 23 September 1947, a proposed FEC policy de-
cision concerning the supply of food for civilian consumption in
Japan.
Paragraph 3 of this paper reads "During the period of acute
food shortage, imports of food should be the minimum required to
prevent such starvation and widespread disease and civil unrest
as would endanger the safety of the occupation forces, and no
imports exceeding this minimum should be permitted which would
have the effect of givin preferential treatment to the Japanese
over the peoples of any Allied Power or liberated area.
The Department of the Army is willing to give its concurrence
0
provided the words "to attain the ob jectives of the occupation and"
are inserted after the words "should be the minimum required.
Without this insertion, in the body of the policy itself,
this jovernment would be sanctioning an FEC policy which might
serve to prevent food importations, above disease and unrest levels,
required in the interests of economic rehabilitation. Acceptance
of FrC-248/4, in its present form could be most embarrassing in the
face of our reco mition -- that only through Japanesee conomic re-
7
covery, sufficient to meet her costs of imports, can the present
U. S. financial assistance to Japan be eliminated.
Also, it is noted, neither the proposed policy nor the U. S.
statement for the minutes, define clearly what the ration level is.
Since food deficiencies for Japan are being met from U. S. sources,
paid for by U. s. funds, it is considered most undesirable that we
place ourselves in the position where SCAP determinations on food
requirements may be subject to question by other governments.
Present requirements for Japan and Korea are in the neighbor
hood of 2,500, 000 tons (wheat equivalent). The Department of the
Army is convinced that at least 2,000,000 tons is required as a very

=== Page 301 === (confidence 0.97)
minimum in wheat and/or flour. Allocations thus far approved by the
U. S. Department of Agriculture have been only 1,500,000. We believe
approval of this paper in its present form would seriously jeopardize
attainment of the minimum considered essential and would probably
lead to criticism by FEC members of even the present entirely inade-
quate allocations. At this particular time approval of this policy
might seriously jeopardize our over-all mission in Japan.
We fully recognize the difficulties which this paper presents
from the point of view of the State Depertment and the Far Eastern
Commission in view of the paper's past history. Homever, at a time
when the Congress is expecting that the program in all of the occupied
areas will be directed toward the development of self-supporting econ-
omies in countries receiving American aid, we believe that it would be
unvise to give approval to policy vhich might well prevent the attain-
ment of this objective.
—Wieean H.Xyraper
William H. Draper, Jr.
Under Secretary of the Army

=== Page 302 === (confidence 0.99)
To:
A-S - Mr. Saltzman
From:
OE - Er. Whitman
Subjeot:
FEC policy for Japanese food imports.
The FEC is about to act on a policy statement on the "Supply of
Food for Civilian Consumption in Japan!. (FEC 248/4, attached as
Enclosure "A") The Department of the Army has requested the state-
ment be amended as follows:
"During the period of acute world shortages, imports of
food for Japan should be the minimum required to attain the ob-
lectives of the occupation and to prevent such starvation and
widespread disease and civil unrest as would endanger the safety
of the occupation forces, and no imports exceeding this minimm
should be permitted which would have the effect of giving pre-
ferential treatment to the Japanese over the peoples of any
Allied Power or liberated area."
This amendment is not acceptable because; (a) it is unnocesaary
for the protection of U.S. interests; (b) it contravenes a long estab-
lished U.S. position; and (c) it would seriously affect our relations
with other powers on the FEC.
U.S. interests are amply protected without the amendment since
SCAP is responsible for estimates of "disease and unrest" requirements
which are flexible, have always been greater than the shipments per-
mitted by the world supply situation, and will continue to be so in
the foreseeable future. Furthermore, the paper is effective only during
the period of acute world food shortage and it is entirely within the
power of the U.S. to determine when this condition exists. So long as
such e condition does in fact exist, it is incongeivable that as a
practical matter the U.S. would in fact avowedly give Japan preferential
treatment over liberated countries. Finally, the U.S. can protect its
interest in Japan in any case through the actions of its representatives
in the IEFC where exporting governments are bound only by allocations
their representatives approve.
The U.S. is committed to the unamended position. It approved the
principle therein on April 25, 1946 when it voted for a previous FEC
policy statement on Food Supply for Japan. On October 31, 1946 SWNCC
again approved the principle and a paper incorporating it was intro-
duced into the FEC. SWNCC affirmed this position in SWNCC 331/3 in
February 1947 (Enclosure B") and reaffirmed its position in FEC.
Finally, the U.S. again indicated its approval of the principle in FEC
in August 1947 when, after receiving Department of the Army conourrence,
it announced its support of the pending FEC paper. A repudiation of
a basic principle supported for 18 months would be serious. The SWNCC
331 series contained two statements which are deleted in the present
FEC 284. They are:
1. "The objectives of relief policy are to prevent such
starvation and widespread disease and civil umrest as would (1)
clearly endanger the occupying forces, and (2) permanently ob-
struct the ultimate objectives of the occupation."
2. "The Supreme Commander should be responsible for pro-
viding imported food supplies only to supplement local resources
and only to the extent supplementation is nesded to prevent such
sidespread

=== Page 303 === (confidence 0.97)
2
widespreed disease or civil unrest as would endanger the cocupying
forces or permanently obstruct the ultimate objectives of the
oocupetion."
These statements were deleted along with other language, parts of which
were contrary to U.S. interests. The omission of these two statements
is not material. The first, limiting feeding, as it does, to the pre-
vention of disease and unrest which would clearly endanger the Occupying
Forces, is less to the War Depertment's interest than the final language
which the War Department wishes to modify. The omission of the phrase
"permanently obstruct" is, if anything, favorable to U.S. interests.
The second provision is only a statement of SCaP responsibility and in
no way affects the privilege of the U.S. to provide for feeding at an
early level than to prevent disease and unrest should it wish to do so.
The proposed amendment would seriously affect oontinued international
cooperation in the Far East. Other countries, especially where there is
a serious food shortage, have been greatly concerned over the food prob-
len. These countries know, and largely accept the fact, that already
Japan is receiving under the disease and unrest formula more food than
some Allied nations. But if the U.S. Governnent should now ask for
J
enough food for Japan to meet the elastic formula, "the objectives of
the occupation" as a priority over their own needs, they would strongly
resent it and might regard it as evidence that this government is attempt-
ing to build up Japan at the expense of the Allies of the U.S. An at-
tempt by the U.S. to repudiate its previous commitments and to urge a
position which is known to be strongly disapproved by other governments
would gravely increase the problem of obtaining their cooperation in
other important matters.

suject: Supply of Food for Civilian Consumption in Japan -
FEC 248/4
The Department of the Army proposes to amend
FEC 243/4 Supply of Food for Civilian Consumption in
Japan as follows:
"3. During the period of acute world shortage,
imports of food for Japan should be the minimum
required to prevent such starvation and widespread
disease and civil unrest as would endanger the
safety of the occupation forces or jeopardize
attainin- the objectives of the occupation, and
no imports exceeding this minimum should be per-
mitted which would have the effect of giving
preferential treatment to the Japanese over the
peoples of any Allied Fower or liberated area."
The history of the attempts of the FEC to develop
a satisfactory policy on import of food for civilian
consumption in Japan is lon and difficult. The first
part of this history is detailed in Appendix "B" of
SWNCC 331, to which I suggest you refer. You should
also note items 7, 8 and 9 in Appendix "A" of SWNCC 331
which indicate the degree to which the United States
1s committed to a policy statement containing the
language of FEC 248/4.
Subsequent to the history outlined in SWNcc 331,
SWNCC approved a new policy statement on food which was
submitted to the FEC on November 12. After a very ex-
tensive discussion the paper was amended by the FEC.
SWiCC approved, with amendments, the revIsed policy
statement. However, no agreement was reached in the
Commission because of a relatively minor last minute
1ssue involving a requirement for publicity to the
alleged fact that Japan was bein: better fed than many
countries which had suffered from Japanese aggression.
Accordingly the proposed paper was tabled by the FEC
in Narch 1947.
In August 1947 the U. K. submitted another paper
(FEC 248) which eliminated all reference to the
1.
controversal

=== Page 308 === (confidence 0.98)
一2-
controversal features in the previous paper which had
caused 1ts non-acceptance by the FEC. After considera-
tion of the revised paper it was determined that the
U. S. could accept it. The War Department approval is
given in a memorandum which I quote in full:
"MEMCRANDUM FOR MR. NOEL HEMMENDINGER, DEPART-
VENT OF STATE
"Subject: Supply of Food for Civilian Relief
in Japan - FEC-248
"1. Per your request of this morning and in
accordance with my telephonic advice to you 26 August,
War Department has no objection to FEC-248."
Accordingly it was indicated in the Steering
Committee of September 23, and the Commission meeting
of October 2, that the U. S. could approve the British
paper as amended (FEC-248/4).
I would like now to comment briefly on the specific
proposal made by Mr. Draper.
1. If the interests of the U. S. are con-
ceived as insuring achievement of our objectives
in Jepan regardless of other U. S. policies and
considerations, the Draper amendment has some
merit since the term "objectives of the occupa-
tion" can be interpreted quite elastically.
Under such a policy the U. S. could ship any
amount of food to Japan that we determined was
proper without being subject to effective chal-
lenge by any other party.
2. This question of the status of the
"objectives of the occupation"in a food paper
has been discussed with the Department of the Army
over the past eighteen months and it has been agreed
that the protection civen by the Draper formula is
not essential to nrotect the interests of the U. S.
Under the "disease and unrest" formula MacArthur
has creat latitude in determinine the minimum
imports to be sent to Japan. As a matter of fact
acArthur has never received the amount of imports
which

=== Page 309 === (confidence 0.98)
一3-
which he has claimed are essential on the
"disease and unrest" basis, and with the cur-
rent food situation probably will not in the
immediate future.
3. The U. S. has agreed to the formulation
of the policy statement without "objectives of
the occupation" in a whole series of documents
going back to the original policy decision by
the FEC approved on April 25, 1946. There 1s
considerable demerit in now attempting to re-
pudiate a previous U. S. position.
4. A proposal to amend the FEC paper by the
addition of the Draper phrase would be strongly
resented by our colleagues since it openly states
that "objectives of the occupation" in Japan as
interpreted by SCAP (and incidentally, of course,
the U. S.) take precedent over requirements of
our Allies. Cur Marshall Plan efforts would be
affected and new amnunition would be provided to
the propaganda campaign which is charging that
the U. S. wishes to build up Germany and Japan at
the expense of our Allies.
5. Other countries feel so deeply on this
issue that I do not believe the Department of
State should even submit the Draper amendment for
Commission consideration. If the U. S. must re-
pudiate its previous position it should merely
take the position that no agreement on the food
paper Is possible and not attempt to debate the
issue.
Our action on this question partly depends on an
appraisal of the effects in the FEc of failure to agree
on a food poliey statement. I have discussed this
matter with  eneral WcCoy who believes that it would
have a serious effect upon efforts to maintain inter-

indicate the same to you in a separate memorandum. OE
supports him in this position. We believe that the
"a' sease and unrest" formula amply protects our Jap-
anese interests and that there are considerable political
risks in the FEC and in Europe from refusing to agree
to a food policy paper.
I see no other alternative but to recommend that
the attempt by the Department of the Army to amend the
previous

be resisted vigorously and effectively repudiated.

ployed.

APPENDIX
FOOD SUPPLY FOR JAPAN
(FEC Policy Decision, Apr1l 25, 1946)
In view of the world food shortage, vhich will be at its most
criticel stege over the next three months, and in view of the conditions
prevailing in the territories of the Allied Powers, the Far Eastern
Comission:
(1) decides as a matter of policy that, except to the extent that
the Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers, with the advice of
the Allied Council for Japan, determines that imports are essential
immediately for the safety of the Occupation Forces, no imports shall
be permitted which vill have the effect of giving to the Japanese a
priority or preferential treatment over the requirements of the peoples
of any Allied Power or libereted aree; and
(2) requests the United States Government, in the light of the
nolicy set out above, inmedietely to review the food import program for
Japan in consultation with the United Nations Relief and Rehabilitation
Administration, the Combined Food Board, and other alloceting
authorities.

=== Page 322 === (confidence 0.99)
ANNEX
Additional Background Material on the Issue
of an FEC Policy for Japanese Food Imports
A. Previous History: For more than a year and a half other members of
the FEC have pressed the United States to agree to a statement of policy
regarding suprly of food for civilian consumption in Jepan. The FEC,
with U.S. concurrence, made a policy decision April 25, 1946 (AppendixD)
that "except to the extent that the Supreme Commender for the Allied Powers,
with the advice of the Allied Council for Jepan, determines that imports
are essential immedistely for the safety of the Occupation Forces, no
imports shall be permitted which will have the effect of giving to the
Japanesf  priority or preferential treatment over the requirements of
the peonles of any Allied Power or liberated area". The United States
regarded t is decision as binding on it but refused to transmit it to
SCAP. The FEC requested the United States to submit a policy paper
which mipht be transmitted to SCAP. Such a paper was approved by SWNCC
October 31, 1946 in SiNCC 331 and introduced into the FEC. This paper
contaired essentially the same principle as that quoted above.
Following discussion in FEC, it was necessary to modify parts of the
paper, which wes done in SWNCC 331/3 in February 1947 (Appendix ), which
reaffirmed the essental provisicn regarding level of feeding. The United
States reaffirmed its suprort of its provision in FEC. In order to pro-
vide the United States with the greatest possible latitude for determin-
ing the food supply to be allowed Jepan, the United States read into the
minutes a statement to be forwarded to SCAP with the policy peper which
crovided that the United States should determine what level of feeding in
eddition to that necessary to prevent disease and unrest etc., could be
allowed without giving preferential treatment to the Japanese. The paper
failed of passage for reasons not meterial to the pr sent issue.
In order to break the deadlock the UK, in August 1947, proposed
FEC 248, now under consideration. After obtaining written concurrence
by the Department of the Army, the State Derartment indiceted in FEC that
it would support the British compronise paper. It is understood by all
concerned that the same statement for the minutes will be accepted in
connection with the present aper. On the morning the paper was to be
approved at top FEC, the Department of the Army stated that it would ob-
ject to the US voting for the paper unless it were made to provide that
the minimun feeding level should be that which not only would prevent
such disease and urnrest as would endanger the safety of the Occupation
Forces but also as would be sufficient to "attain the objectives of the
occupation". The US member of FEC wos therefore directed to defer a vote.
B. Department of Army Arguments: 1. The policy would jeopardize obtain-
ing immediately neeied imports. The Department of the Army states that it
believes

=== Page 323 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
believes that present requirements for Japan and Korea are in the
neighborhood of 2,500,00c tons (wheat oquivalent), that Agriculture
has approved only 1,500,000 and that approval of the paper in its
present form would seriously jeopardize atteinment of the minimum
considered essental, and might seriously jeopardize our over-all mission
in Japan.
The requirement of 2,500,COC tons is based on SCAP's disease and
unrest request. As the Department of the Army indicates, it will be
impossible to meet this figure. The problem however is the short
world supply, competing demands, and the toughness of the Department
of Agriculture. These factors will not be affected by any FEC action.
2. The policy would hold back economic rehabilitation in Japan.
The Department off the Army argues (Appendix B ) in support ofits position
that without inserting this language in the body off the policy itself,
this Government would be sanctioning an FEC policy which might serve
to prevent ffood importations above disease and unrest levels required in
the interests of economic rehabilitation, and it would be embarrassing
in the face of our recognition that only through Japanese economic
recovery, sufficient to meet her costs of imports, can the present U.S.
financial assistance to Japen be eliminated.
The Department of State does not dispute the need of larger food
imports to obtain fu'l economic recovery in Japan. But it also sees the
need for food imports to obtain full economic recoveryin other key parts
of the world. When we are seeking a world economic recovery there is no
inconsistency in stating that Japan's share of scarce imports must be
determined in the light of other requirements. In fact, it would be
inconsistent to be seeking food and financing for food imports for France,
Netherlandsetc. and at the same time announcing that Japan's requirements
had preference over these countries.
C. FEC Considerations: The importance of the maction of FEC countries
to the Army amendment must be judged in light of our general world
objectives, the negotiation on the Peace Treaty problem and the need
for international co-operation if Japan is to be rehabilitated. There
is no question as to the violence of the FEC reaction, as indicated
in Generel McCoy's letter (Apnendix C). He particularly stresses two
points: 1. The mmendment contravenes the the long-established U.S.
nosition taken in FEC. As stated above the U.S. has anrroved the principle
objected to by the Department of the Army on three different occasions in
the FEC before the introduction of the present paper by the U.K., and has
indicated its approval of the current paper in workin committees. To
suggest, and certeinly to insist on, the introduction of the Department
of the

=== Page 324 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
of the Army's proposal a ter this history would tend to impair confidence
of the FEC countries in the integrity of the U.S.
2. The substance of the amendment is offensive to the FEC. Other
countries, especially those with food shortages, have been greatly con-
cerned over the food problem. These countries know and largely accept
the fact that, under the disease and unrest formula, the Japanese are
already being fed at a level higher than some of the people of some of the
Allied nations. However, if the U.S. Govetnment should now ask for enough
food for Japan to meet the elastic formula "the objectives of the occupa-
tion" during a period of acute food shortage as a preference or priority
over the needs of the neighboring liberated Allied countries, they would
strongly resent it and might regard it as evidence that this Government
is attempting to build up Japan at the expense of the Allies of the
United States.
D. Terminal Date for the Policy: The Department of State has never con-
sidered it necessary to have a terminal date on the policy for the follow-
ing reason. SCAP, who, the FEC agrees, will be a United States Officer,
is responsible under the policy statament for estimates of "disease and
unrest" requirements, which are flexible. These requirements have always
been greater than the shipments permitted by the world supply situation,
and will continue to be so in the foreseeable future. The paper is effec-
tive only during the neriod of acute world food shortage, and it is entirely
within the power of the U.S. to determine when this condition ends. So
long as such condition does in fact exist, it is inconceivable as a practi-
cal matter that the U.S. would in fact avowedly give Japan preferential
treatment. Finally, the U.S. can protect its interests in food supnly to
Japan through the actions of its representatives in the IEFC where, as
the principal exporting government to Japan, it will be bound only by
allocations its representatives approve.
Nonetheless the Department in an effort to meet the Department of
the Army part way, and to make absolutely sure our long-run interests are
not injured, offered to accept the policy enly with a terminal date. While
this too might make for erious argument at the FEC, General McCoy ultimately
has agreed to propose it. The position of the Department of the Army in
refusing this compromise is difficult to understand.
E. Relation to Previous Policy Proposals on Same Subiect: FEC 24&, now
under consideration, deleted a number of paragraphs of SWNcc 331/3. In
authorizing the U.S. member in FEC to proceed with FEC 248, State, with
Department of the Army concurrence, ∞nsidered that these deletions were
not injurious to U.S. interests. Army now argues that the deletion of two
of them might be injurious. They are:
1. "The objectives of relief policy are to prevent such
starvation and widespread disease and civil unrest as would (1)
clearly

=== Page 325 === (confidence 0.98)
-4-
clearly endanger the occupying forces, and (2) permanently ob-
struct the ultimate objectives of the occupation."
2. "The Supreme Commander should be responsible for pro-
viding imported food supplies only to supplement local resources
and only to the extent supplementation is needed to prevent such
widespread disease or civil unrest as would endagner the occupying
forces or permanently obstruct the ultimate objectives of the
occupation."
These statements were taken from earlier directives to SCAP which were
intended to restrict SCAP's and U.S. responsibility for imports to the
minimum necessary for Japanese needs. (Note the emphasis on "clearly
endanger" and "permanently obstruct".) Furthermore, paragraphs 2 and 3
of SWNCC 331/3, which are in essence the same as FEC 248, clearly embraced
these provisions and were intended to be the effective language during
acute world food shortage.
C. E. Saltsman

"Mr. Chairman, may I be permitted to make some statements
on the general aspeot of this paper now that it 1s going to be
passed? This 1s not the first occasion on whioh the Far Eastern
Commission has been called upon to consider the qaestion of food
supplies for Japan. The Commission will recall the very keen
1nterest whioh my Gevernment, and particularly my Minister, Sir
Carl Berendsen, has taken in the matter in its eamlier stages,
and I don't have any desire at all to recapitulate any part of
the diffioult and prolonged disoussions which we have had on this
paper extending almost for over a year -- well over a year.
"I am instruoted by my Government to support the present
paper as being the best obtainable under the circumstanees,
but I should like to comment on one or two of the general
aspeote involved.
"Or course, we realize that it 1s entirely natural and
understandable that an occupying power holding suoh a pre
dominant place in Japan as does the United States should
earnestly desire to avoid any unnecessary complication of its
diffioukt and delicate task. It is also true that it 1s
difficult to build a democrstio country out of a hungry
people. These things are plain and undeniable.
"But there 1s another prinoiple involved. If there is
to be hardship, and ir at a time like this when there 1s not
food enough to go around, anyone has to go short, then clearly,
as we see it, the shortage and the suffering should fall upon
the aggressors and not upon their victims. There does not
seem to be any reason whatever, in loglo or in equity, why,
for example, the people or China, or Malaya or the Philippines
or India or Burma, or, indeed, any of the peoples of the world
who are suffering from hunger as a direot result of Japanese
aggresslion, should be allowed to go short of food in a time
of desperate need while the Japanese, the substantial cause of
their mlseries, are supplied with food in greater proportion.
The paper before us acknowledges this general principle, the
validity of which, of course, is unquestionable, but the paper
provides an important exception, in thet the Japanese may be
permitted to receive more food than their former viotims if
suoh extra food is necessary to prevent suoh starvation and
widespread disease and civil unrest as will endanger the
safety of the occupation forces. This 1s an exception, sir,
which may become both unjust and dangerous.
"It may become unjust because it oan lead to a vlolation
of that general prinoiple I have referred to, andit may be-
come dangerous because it leaves the way open for the Japanese,
by deliberately

Enclosure to Despatch No. 131 dated January 19, 1948,
from American Embassy, London, England.
Source: The Economist
January 17, 1948
FOOD AID POLITICS IN JAPAN
Japan's new census, taken in October, 1947, has
shown a total population of 7S million as compared with
69 million for the present area in the census of 1935.
Thus, in spite of heavy military and civilian war
casualties, the population has increased by 9,000,000
in twelve years, partly by natural growth and partly as
a result of the compulsory repatriation of Japanese
nationals from Korea, Formosa, China and elsewhere since
the end of the war. This population cannot be supported
exclusively on food produced within Japan; a substantial
amount of loodstuffs must in any case be imported and
paid ror either from the proceads of foreign trade or by
credit or subsidy from abroad--probably about lo per cent
of the total required to prevent widespread malnutrition.
However, thou h it is impossible to dispense with food
imports, it is very important for Japan to obtain the
maximum or food production at hone, bot! in order to
reduce the prosent adverse balance of trade, which
throatens inoustry with paralysis from lack of raw mat-
erials, and in orde? to satisfy the Amaricans, whose aid
is sourht, that the Japanese are loiny the best they can
for thomselves.
Fut Japan's canacity for focd production and the

I. GENERAL. STATEMENT: Evaluation of the Japanese Food Situation.
Japan, historically, hs been a food deficit country. The r has not areated but only has mpbasised
a situation that will contimue to be one of Japan's mjor problems. Japan proper, with a populatiom of 79
million, has a total land area less than that of the state of california, md of that area only l6 paromt
is arable. Through the intensive utilization of all her resources, Japan has held her iaports of foed over
the last twenty years dom to an average of 3 million tons a year and has met approximately 85 paroent of
her food roquirements from indigenous production. Japan' 1mport of food prior to Soptber 1945 cam
principaily from Formosa, foroa, and Manchuriz. The conclusion of the war out off those souroes of upply.
During the war imports averaged 3 million tons; rice production remained cloee to the prewar average.
Importa of rice from 1936 to 19 averazed :.9 million tons; production of rice for the a period avreraged
9.7 million tons, far a tota: effective supply of nearly 12 million tons. In 1945, food imports droppod to
1.5 million tons, rice production to 6.4 miilion tons. The reduction in crop production in the fall of 1945
and the low le ve: of food imports during :946 (65c, (c0 tons through the ond of cctober) remulted in the most
surious food crisis in Japan's history. The effective supply available for rationing during the l946 rice
year (November 194.5 - (ctober 1946 was augmented by various xpedionta. 320,000 tan from military stock-
pile were conauned, as we:l as i94,( tons of running tock or reserves. A rovised colloction policy for
the l946 sumer cros, limitin: far retention of thoe orope to the quantitie required for consumption
prior to the fall rice harvest (instead of s; reading wheat and barley retention over a twelve month period),
increased tho pply of summer grains available for rationin. Abnormally rapid conaumption of the l946
rice and swoet potato crops, aided by an eariy harvest, made a subatantial contribution to the maintenance
of ration distritution in Se;tamber and  ctober, 1946.
The fall harvest of 19.b produced a rice crop catimated at 9.2 million otric tons, and an excellent
wert potato cro;, a considerable increasc over the previous harvest. Unusually rapid consumption of the
1946 fali cros and certain other factors acted, however, to limit the increase in sffective supply avail-
able for rationins during the 1947 rice year (Noveber 1 46 - Gctober 1947). (n the other hand, rationing
requirementa wure larger than in 194o, mainiy as a rosu.t of the repatriation of 3.8 million Japanese, and
of the establis:ment of a slisntly higner and somomt.at more roaliatic, though still insufficient, staple ra-
tion of lt calorios. Althougn the net effect was a rationing deficit in 1947 of approximately the same
magnitude as that experienced duriny ., the curtaiiment was in the slightly more adoquate staple ration
of 1o calories, as compared to the tly unrealistic staple ration of 42 calories prevailing in l946.
Further, the sup: lies avai:anle in I a? made it : ossible to min:mize tm adverse effect of the food deficit
on production in certain rey industries, us;cially coa: and fertilirer, to a greater rxtent than had been
possible durir, the firs: year of te  ccujatio.. Nevert:eless, the food situation in 1947, as in 194o, was
characterired uy serious . a ax: curtaiiment in ration distribution during the aprin and summer months
with tie usual adverse economic, socia) ane nutritiona: onse puences ( a failure to mect an established ra-
tion.
1

=== Page 357 === (confidence 0.98)
It is important that the consequences of the food shortage be evaluated in relation to the economio
climate of which the food problem constitutes a critical elemant. The price of rice has been a significant
barometer of the rising trend of prices, in both the official and the blackmarket. The official purchase
price of rice established in November l945 was one yen per kilogram, approximately 5 times the price pre-
vailing in the 1934-36 base period. The official price established in october for the 1947 crop ws nearly
twelve yen per kilogram-approximately 63 times the base period and a twelve-fold increase in tmo years.
This was based on a parity index reflecting the increase in official prices of important comodities pur-
chased by farmers and was representative of the general movement of official prices.
Food costs, representing about 7C percent of the consumer
budget in urban areas, have been by far the
most important factor underlying demands for wage increases
which, in tun, have produced highor labor
costs. All efforts at wage and price stabilization operate under considerable difficulty because of the
pressure to secure food from other than official channels while the ration remains at the below-eubsistance
lovel of 1246 calories.
The relationship of food to the volume of industrial production must not be overlooiad. The position
of almost complete stoppage of Japanese industry shortly aftor the end of the war was largely the result of
the chaos caused by the dislocations of the war and the shock of the surrender. in January, l946, industri-
al production stood at less than 20 percent of the 193(-34 average and loss tha:. 10 percent of the war-tims
peak. During 1946 and 1947 the level of industrial production increased to something over 40 percant of the
1930-34 average, r 20 percent of the war-tine peak. Although the shortage of basic raw materials, both in-
digenous and imported, is the most important factor underlying the continuing low level of industrial pro-
duction, the low food ration is of almost equa: impor'ance. The inflationary situation increases greatiy
the difriculty of collecting maximum quantities of food from producers. The farmerst inability to secure
consumer goods, the shortage of incentive goods, and the differential between official and blackmarket food
prices encourage withholding of foodstuffs by farmers to tarter for desired goods or for sale at higher than
le,al prices.
Scarcity of rationed food affects roduction adversely in other ways. The lowered vitality of labor a
a result of a sub-standard diet reduces ;er capita output and, in turn, raises unit cost. The supplementary
rations for heavy wo: kers in essential industries, wtdch nave lessened the detrimental effect of the food
shortage on industrial production, have only provided a partial solution, however, due to the limitations on
the program imposed by the overall food shortage. Absentecism, resulting: from laborers going on food forag-
ing expeditions to secure supplemental food, has been an additional factar retarding the industrial revival.
The Japanese economy depends for healthy survival on the importation of food and raw materials and the ex-
portation of manufactured products at a level sufficient to finance the imports required to support a rea-
sonable standard of living. A relatively stable price level would facilitate ef orts to revive Japants for-
eign trade. lack of exports in suf:icient quantity to obtain foreign exchange with which to purchaso food
and industrial raw materials in juantitics necessary to meet Japan's minimum needs helps maintain the infla-
tionary circle by prolonging the scarcities which are the fundamental cause of the inflation.
Past experience ciearly indicates that a minimum condition for preventing further sorious deterioration
2

=== Page 358 === (confidence 0.98)
of the economy is the maintenance and regular distribution of the present ration allowenees. The rational
distribution of a minims subsistence ration is the logical approach to check the rising tempo of the infla-
tionary spiral. Past experience has further demonstrated that a shortfall in food import arrivals and the
consequent failure to maintain distribution of the established ratiou level has a deleterious sffect on the
entire economy of Japan out of all proportion to the quantities involved in the shortfall. In the major
cities of Tokyo and Osaka the cost of living, as reflected in family expenditures, increased by 70 and 50
percent respectively between April and July, largely as a reauit of an increase in staple food expenditures.
This sharp rise was coincident dth the shortfall in rations.
The consequences, then, of short imports are both direct with respect to th health of large segments
of the population, and indirect with respect to the repercuasions upon the Japanese economy. Since Japan
has been living on restricted rations for at least six years, physical reserves are low. Without imported
foods, consumption in urban areas would have been at starvation levels in the spring and summer of l947.
Nutrition survays in Tokyo in Vay and August, for example, indicate that the total food consumption during
those months would have been only 819 and 917 calories per capita per day if imported food had not been
available for distribution. Even wdth the imports provided, avarage consumption was below a temporary main-
tenance level. The less diroct consequences arise from the decreased efficiency and lowored output of a
population underfed and sutject to anxieties and general unrest due to food shortages, and from the insta-
bility in the economy resulting from the inflationary aspecta of ddding in the blackmarket for food when
rations are inadequate.

II. THE 1947 HICE YEAR: 1 November 1946 through 31 Cctober 1947
A. Food Production
1. Staple Food Crops: Cereals and potatoes
Favoratle growing conditions and freedom from crop dazage due to floods or typhoons resulted in a mub-
stantial increase in agricultural production in 1946 over 1945. The rice crop harvested in the fall of 1946
equalled y.2 million metric tons, ∞paring favorably with norzal rice production. A bumper sweet potato
crop alsc was harvested. The i9t.7 suzmer crcps of wheat, : arleys and white potatoes wore adversely affected

2. Other Foods: Fish, Vegetables, Fruits and Weat
During the ;rewar years (19i5-1939 the annua) fish roduction of Jap an ;roper, including all marine
products, ranged from 4.2 to ..5 πillion mtric tons, accountin: for atout 35 percent of the world sup: ly.
pesides being a vital facter in Japan's internationa! trade, fisn represented an indispensable scurce of
food for tie Japanese population. In the Japanese diet fish took the place occupied ty moat in hestern
4

=== Page 360 === (confidence 0.98)
diets and represented 90 percent of the animal protein intake, 12 percent of the fat intake, and 4 peroent
of the caloric intaks. It is estimated that during normal times the average daily per capite consumption of
marine products was 80 grams in terms of fresh fish. Although this did not bulk so large in terms of quan-
tity, the protein and fat provided thereby was, and is, highly essential in the preponderantly cereal diet.
During the wr and the first months of the Occupation, fish production fell off considerably due to re-
striction of fishing grounds, destruction and conversion of fishing boats, extreme shartages of fuel oil and
fishing materiala and lack of skilled manpower. The fishing industry recovered rapidly, however, and de-
spite an ever present critical shortage of fishing materials,the catch is now approaching normal prewar
proportions. It is estimated that production of marine products during the l947 rice year ws approximately
3 million metric tons. This catch should have provided a distribution of about 6C gram per person per
day assuming that the increase in population is offset somewhat by the virtual elimination of fish exports
and the substantial reduction in the quantity of fish used as a raw material for fertilizer and fish oil
manufacture.
Tie most effective utilization of fish production has been limited by lack of sufficient processing
equipant and refrigeration, particularly important during peak runs. During l947  large scale shipbuild-
ing program, sponsored by ScAp, restored the fNshing fleet to an appreciable extent. Fuel oil has been
available in relatively adequate quantities. The allotment of fusl oil to the fishing floet in l947 to-
talled 343,000 kilolitore as compared to 218,000 in 1946. Actual deliveries during 1946, according to the
Ministry of Agriculture and Forestry were 19l,576 kiloliters. Further increases in the fishing fleet with-
out increasing the fishing areas available to the Japanese would probably not increase overall production.
A threat to fish production is lack of ropes and nets in particular and replacement parts in general. The
offect of sortages of nets, tnine and rope became evident in the latter part of l947 If sufficient -
terial is not provided to help maintain fishing operations, the decrease in catch in the twelve-month pariod
of 1948 may amount to 25 percent, arapproximately ec,000 metric tons.
1
Reliable statistics on vegetable production, both prewar and today, are practically unavailable. The
many varieties of vegetables, their differing growing seasons, and the hundreds of thousands of small pro-
ducers make any accurate c∞mpilation impossible. It has been estimated that the annual prewar production,
excluding swect potatoes and white potatoes which are now considered as staple foods, was about 6.5 million
motric tons. This would have provided the population with approximately 2o0 grams per person per day, as-
suming an equal distribution.
Vegetable production during the 1947 rice year has been estimated variously at about 6 to 8 million
metric tons. Factors inhibiting ve, etable production are the increased emphasis on staple food production
and the shortage of conmercial fertilizer as well as insecticides.
Fruit production during the 1947 rice year was estimated at about 52 percent of prewar production.
Meat as a source of nourishment has been unimportant in Japan. Even before the war, a in most Ori-
ental ccuntries, meat production was negligible due to lack of natural pasture land and the necessity to
utilize land for production of crops of high caloric value for direct human consumption. The number of pigs
in 1946 had, decreased 90 percent from the prewar (1935-39) level. The number of cattle in 1946 and 1947 was

4. Deterrents to Colloction
The collection program on 1946 crops was conducted aaidst a number of obetacles. Adequate statistieal
data enabling the assigent of equitable quotas did not exist, and the prinsiple of fair quotas  alleg-
adly violated at all levels. A strong dasire axisted among farmere for blackmarket food sales to finance
purchases of scarce items through illegal channels.
Relative price movemnts and the difficulty of controlling non-ataple foods and certain industrial
items wore such as to militate strongly against voluntary staple food deliveries. Diffioulties in mintain-
ing industrial production schedules and tranaportation programs retarded the delivery of even the amall
quantity of incentive goods which had been promised to farmors (Tables 17, 18). Purther, the movement of
repatriates to rural villages resulted in increased pressure upon farmer retention stocks.
The political atmosphere was not conducive to maximum fod oollections. prefectural administrrtions
were often temporary in nature, and officials were increasingly aware of the axpanded degree of local auto-
nosy which would soon prevail. Farmers believed that the democratisation program would imediately bettur
their traditionally disadvantageous pocition. This emotional framework, in coinoidonce with the series of
spring elections, created an exoellent opportunity for repeated mphasis on the "righte" of farmers in re-
spet to their produce and to inare or misstate their less pleasant food delivery obligation. propkganda
in support of partial or non-delivery of foodstuffs was not uncommon, and it s often couched in language
wmich rendered suppreasion difficult.
Continuous efforts were made by SCAf and the Japanese Government to offset the factors aeting as deter-
rents to maximum food colloctions. Action was taken during l947 to increase the aocaraoy of production es-
timates by the institution of a revised crop reporting system utilizing crop roporters mployed by the Cen-
tral Governaent instead of prefectural officials who are subject to local pressures to underestimate produs-
tion. The greatest possible use was made of the "linked" distribution of farm necessities to secure ful-
fillment of ∞llection quotas. The legal basis for compulsory ∞llections was expanded to include rice sub-
atitutes specifically. Active surveillance by Military Governmant teams was highly effective in offsetting
the tendency toward sectionalism and the subordination of national food collection and distribution plans to
local. interesta. The actual quantity of staple food collected for controlled distribution during the l947
rice crop year may be considered remarkable, particularly in view of the impact of generally unfavorable
economic conditions upon farmmrs, and the concurrent development of mcre nearly sutonomous prefectural and
local government  part of th broad democratization program.

C. Pood Distribution
The Japanese diet is a comparatively simple one - consisting mainiy of rice and other cereals, pote-
toes, fish, vegetables, miso (soy bean paste), shoyu (soy sauce), and edible oil. Meat and driry products
play an inmignificant part in the diet in Japan as woll as in other Criental countries since the production
of grain for direct human consumption yields a much greater caloric output than the utilisation of land for
the production of grain for animal feed.
All major foode are rationed in Japan. The food ration consiste of staple foods (rice, other cereals,
and potatoss), miso, shoyu, edible oil and fsh and regotables. The ration of fish and vegetables is not
fixed since it varies with the seasona and location. The oi! ration has been negligible, each person re-
ceiving a ration approximately twice a year. The distribution of miso and
shoyu has averaged about 43 per-
cent and 68 percent respectively of the nominal rations of these
itams.

The inflationary impact of the rati n curtailment during the late spring and summer months of l947 has

2. Ferishable Foods
Equitable distritution of fisn in Japan is of an importance far out of proportion to the caloric intake
which it provides. In terms of nourishment, fish provides the bulk of the animal protein in the Japanese
must be availabic at prices within reach of the iowest income class. Therefore, fish is rationed in Japan
despite the serious difficulties which are inevitably enccuntered in attemptin, to ration a product of this
nature. Some of the inherent problems are:
(1) The perishable nature of the comaodity does not permit the delays ncident to the administra-
tion of a rationin system.
(2) Fair prices are difficult to eatablish due to the more than 4tC arieties of adible Japanese
fish and tho grades witrin these varicties. The de ree of freshness, which would normally be a vital factor
in pricinu: a lot of fish in a open market, carmot be considered in fixing official maximum prices.
(3) Cold storage, refrigerated cars, and other preservation facilit ies in Japan were never suffi-
cient and ar now wholly inadequate, fre uently rndering a wide-spread equitable distribution of the catch
impasitle.
(4) The extreme shortage of nets and other fishing gear forces fishermen to purchase poor grade
14

=== Page 370 === (confidence 0.97)
subetitutes at high blackmarket pricas. Consequently, there is a strong tendency for fishermen to eell
their catch at high blackaarket prices in order to offset those expenditures.
These and other related problems cannot entirely be solved under present economic conditiona. But de-
apite the many factors impeding the officient execution of the fish distribution system, during the 1947
retion of 33 grams per person per day at official maximum prices.
In April l947, after months of intensive study of the fish distribution problams by the Japanese Eco-
nomic Stabilization Board, tho inistry  Agriculture and Porestry and interested SCAp agencies, a com-
pletely revised distributi n system was inaugurated. The new system provided tighter control and an im-
proved "link" administration while eliminatin, undesirable monopolistic control. Investigation is continu-
ing and offorts are conatantly being made to increase th flow of fish
through official distribution chan-
nolo.
With equal distribution of the estimated vegetable production of Jajan l60 grams of vegetables should
have been available to each person per day. The quantity actually distributed through ration channels to
food deficient areas 1s estimated to be as low as a daily average of l00 grams par capita. Although this
represents only a few calories, the vitamin and mineral content is indispensable in the Japamese diet.
With nocessary changes due to the nature of the commodity, the vegetable collection, distribution and
rationing system closely parallels that already outlined for fish. The problems rendering effective distri-
bution machinory difficult are also much the same: the highly perishable nature of the comodity, eodstance
of innumerable amall scale producers, seasonable nature of the supply, and the many varieties and grades.
Since emphasis must neoessarily be placed on the high calorie staple foods, almost all agricultural "link"
commodities available are turned over to the staple food producars, thus depriving vegetable farmere of the
most effective incentive toward supi lying official channels.
In August l947, the vegetatle rationing syatem was revised and responaibilities for the various distri-
bution steps were more definitely fixed. Distribution procedures were made more specific.
Wh: le there was a nominal distribution control f fruit during 1946-47, it was so ineffective that in
October 1947 the control was lifted and fruit was permitted to circulate on the freo market. This action
was taken in order to concentrate control offorts on other more cssential commodities and because fruit is
primarily a luxury article in the Japanese diet.

The basic ingrecient of miso and shoyu is soy beans - an important source of protein in the prewar
Japanese diet. In the years 1935-39 more than a million tons of soy beans were consumd in Japan annually -
approximately 70 percent of these supplies were imported from Manchuria and Korea. For the most part, the
soy boans grown in Japan, roughly 325,000 metric tons were used by the farmers for making miso, shoyu and
tofu (bean curd) on the farm and for feed and fertilizer. Soy beans wsed in commeroially produced commodi-
ties such as miso and shoyu were 8C-90 percent imported in 1935-39, and during the war years these food i-
tems were produced almost entirely from imported beans. Imports actually exceeded 930,000 mtric tons in
1945 (927,000 from Manchuria alone), but much of this  used a a staple food substitute.
Efforts to import soy beans from Manchuria are being made continuously, but recaipts have been negligi-
ble. Since the war Korea has been a net food-deficit area and has been unable to ship soy beans to Japan.
Indigenous production in Japan in the 1946 and 1947 rice ys has been 250-300,000 metric tons-fst slight-
ly below prewar--but since most farmers produce soy beans on a small scale to oonsume rether than sell, only
40-50,oco metric tons of this has been sold far processing into miso, shoyu and oil. pulse imports--chiefly
from the United States--during the past two years (through Sept. 1947) totaled about 190,000 mtric tons,
including 60,000 metric tons of peas and 70,000 metric tons of soya flour. About 150,000 mtric tons of the
190,o00 metric ton total have been released as a staple food substitute.
There is very little sugar produced in Japan proper. During the prewar period 1935-39, an average of
approximately 1,000,00 metric tons of sugar was imported aually, more than 75 percent of mhich came from
Formosa. In the same period, annual indigenous production averaged 40,000 metric tons-less than 4 percent
of the total supply. Indigenous production during the war was cut to about l0,o00 mstric tons per year by a
land utilization program which emphasized the production of staple food crops at the expense of vegetables
and sugar beets. The land utilization program, of necessity, still encourages staple foods, and sugar pro-
duction during the first two years of the Occupation has remained at the war-time level of about l0,0o0 met-
ric tons per year. The continuing fertilizer shortage has made increased yields virtually impossible, but

III. PUBLIC HEALTH ASPECTS OF THE POOD SHORTAGE
The nutritional status of the Japanese people as indieated by changes in body wmight recorded in mutri-
tional surveys made every three months on 15c,000 people, reached a low point in Augmst 1946 at the and of
the first year o: the Occupation. Since kugust l946, some improvement has been indicated by the docrease in
the percentage of individuals wdth one or mare clinical symptons associated dth mutritional deficiencies,
although the survey in August 1947 showed a slight increase over May 1947 in the proportion of persons wdth
such sympt.
Coincidont with the rice and sweet potato narvest in November 1946, caloric intakos in all cities in-
creased over the low level prevailing in the summsr and fall of 1946. A slight decrease occurred in Fehrua-
ry 1947. A mubstantial drop in food intake was indicated in the May 1947 survey which reflected the cur-
tailmant of rations in certain areas and the reduced stocks of food available from blackmarkst sources. The
August l947 nutritional survey indicated a further decrease in caloric intake, and an increase in the per-
centage of he population dth body weights at least 10 percent below normal nd with other symptoms of mel-
nutrition. (See Tables 24, 26, 32).
The dependence of the Japanese upon grain products and potatoes for their energy, and the moager quan-
tities of animal products, soy beans, sugar and fat in their diet is shon by the data amalyzing the quanti-
ties of various classes of food consumed. (See Tables 25, 27, 29).
The rural population, in relation to their normal consumption, have lived at a moderately low but even-
ly distributed food intake level throuphout the year. A definite increase in caloric intake was noted in
November 1946, resulting, as in the cities, from the sweet potato harvest. Consumption of grain products
was not increased although increased supplies were available to farmers. There was a slight decrease in
February 1947, and a further decrease in caloric intake in May 1947. (See Table 25).
Phyaical examinations during the warst periods of food restriction show an increase in the proportion
of individuals with symptoms associated with nutritional deficiencias. The May l947 survey showed slight
increases in the individuals, in both city and rural areas, dth edema, loss of kmee jerk, and bradycardia.
The August survoy showed further increases. These symptoms are frequently assoaiated with a vitamin B com-
plex deficiency. There is a seasonal drop in the vitamin B content of rice and a lack of supplamontary
foods, especially vegetables, in the winter which may have been as much a causs of the increase in symptons
as the decreased food supply. (See Tables 32, 33).
The Japanese people have boen on a limited food consumption for a pariod of at least six years and as a
result, are showing the mental lethargy and inability to carry out prolonged physiaal labor characteristic
of chronic malnutrition. A study made on the labor output in relation to food conoumption among coal miners
has showm that the tonnage por man dropped from 14 tons per month to 5.3 tons, as the caloric intake drop-
psd. The nutritional doficiencies of the Japanese population are the result of the unbalnced diat and the
dependenco upon cereal foods as well as the restricted caloric intake. In the rural population the consump-
tion of animal protein averages less than 7 grams per person ;er day, and in the urban population, . grams
per poraon per day; as contrasted to the noreal requirements of 20 grams per person per day. Purther evi-
dence of this state of chronic inadequate nutrition is shomm in the high suscoptibility of the Japsnese
19

=== Page 375 === (confidence 0.98)
a
people during this period to any disease, particularly chronic tuberculosis. The long range consequances of
the continued food shortage will be most acute for the vulnerable categories such as infants and children.
The growth rate in children, as shom in extensive wurveys of school children has been retarded both in
height and weight. As a resuit urban school children are apprcximately one year behind the noral rate of
growth in their bodily developmant; the average twelve year old of today corresponds in height and weight to
the eloven year old child of ten years ago.
Efforts to reduce the effects of chronic malnutrition among children have been undertakon by SCAP, with
the inauguration of a "achool lunch program" which began in January 1947. At present, this provides supple-
mental focd particularly of the types mcst lacking in the diet of these children; namely, animal protein and
calcium. As of May 1947, this program reached 5,486,41e children, attending 3,548 scho0la in 200 cities,
and 4,470 schools in village areas. The ultimate goal of this program, if sufficient quantities of dry skim
milk can be imported and other supplemental items can be obtained, i to include some 13, 000,000 lower
achool children and 5,O0c,000 middle school children.
The nutritional status of the Japanese people reflects an inade quate official food ration and the ex-
ceedingly restricted supply of food on the "free list", i.e. available from food items which are not under
rationing controls. The staple food ration, which initially included only rice, has been progressively
broadened to include wheat, barley, white potatoes, sweet potatoe:, and finally miscellaneous cereals and
coarse grains. In comtination, these foods emtrace approximately e5% cf the total production of calories,
und their inclusion in the staple ration tas reduced sharply the suply of supplementary foods which would
otherwise e available outside tne basic ration.
The schedu:ed staple food ration level for the normal consumer of 1246 calories is, of course, the
major source of calori ir the 'apanese diet. It is supplemented by rations of soy paste, soy sauce, edi-
Lie oil, fiah and vegetables which in aggregate provide an additional 100 to 150 calories per person per
day. This food rationing lovel of 1350 to 1400 calories is considerably
below a minimum subsistence diet,
and any delay or curtailent in ration distritution increases the margin
between the official ration level
and minimum nutritional requirements. Some familie: are able to sup lement the inadequate official ration
by produce from home gardens, but the majrity of the urban popuiation has been dependent upon certain quan-
tities of food purchased outside of official ration channels. The data on actual food consumpticn, as ob-
tained from nutritional survoys, show that the official rati n is supplemented to a considerablo extent by
purchases of fruit and other minor items on the free market and by purchases of staple or other rationed
focds from non-rationed sources. (Tables 24, 26, 28 and 31). This additional food in non-rationed channels
represented a marginal increment which the Governent, despite all efforts, was not able to collect at offi-
cial prices. Although the source of part of this marginal increment may be attributed to food in the hands
of farmera who failed to fulfill their collection quotas, and to the margin of error involved in estimating
crop production for 5 million farming units and the resuitant errors in assignment of collection quotas, to
a considerable extent it is a resu't of the reduction in food consumption by food producers below their ac-
customed dietary level under the pressure of blackmarket prices or barter offers of scarce consumer goods
and family heirlooms. This reduction in food consumption belon an accustomed level is demonstrated by the
20

=== Page 376 === (confidence 0.96)
restricted food consumption by the rural population, particularly as roflected in the pebruary, May, and
August survey data. (Table 2e). Despite the supplementary purchases by the urtan population of free market
or blacimarket food, they have not attained a norma! dietary level, and as noted above there has besn wide-
spread evidence of loss in labor efficiency and malnutrition as a result of the restricted food intake. The
effect of the effort of the urban population to achieve an ac ustomed dietary level has, homever, created an
intense demand for food fro non-rationed sources, the inflationary effects of which have been described in
Section 1 above.

IV. JAPAN'S LON: TERM POCD PROSPECTS
During the summer of 1947, the Japanese Government prepared a comprehensive program designed. to ensure
the maxiram production of staple food crops in the immediate future. This program inoluded a law deedned
to require the planting of a aximm area to staple food production and provided an incentive plan to en-
courage staple food production. The law had not been passed by the Diet by the middle of April l948.
The law and supporting incentive plan provide that pre-planting acreage, production and collection quo-
tas will be assigmed to farmers by the Ministry of Agriculture and farestry through prefectural and village
agricultural committees. The Ministry anticipates that tiia mothod of setting production and collection
quotas will result in greater equity and that the money and incentive goods bonuses for collection in exoess
of quotas will provide an incentive by way of scarce goods and premium prices to induce farmere to maimise
their production of staples.
Part of the future increase in staple food production which may be possible depends on extension of
area planted, largely through a land reclamation program involving 1,500,000 hectares by 1960. There is
some question whether this goal can be fully attained by that date. Half the area involved lies in Hokkai-
do, a region presenting many special problems: inducements to new settlers and farmers to migrate; trans-
portation facilities; capital requirements for draft animals, farm equipment, etc.; and agricultural educa-
tion programs. It is clear that only with a positive plan, and offective educational, administrative, and
financial support by the public authorities is such a land (and regional) development project likoly to be
achieved.
Regardless of the success of reclamation projects and possible expansion of fishing grounds, at the
present rate of population increase of 1,000,000 a year Japan's position as a permanent food deficit country
remains unchanged. In the long run, population incroase will negate current nd potential increases in food
production. The table below indicates approximate staple food requirements at various ration lovels from
1948 through 1953. The 1,685 caloric staple food ration level in 1953 is related to an estimated total
caloric intak: from all foods of approximately 2,175 caloris.

1300 calories. The determining factors, ir. Alber states, will be the
success of the present program to increase Japanese food production by
ten per cent this year anu the successful collection of the pre-planting
quotas which have been established.

price and distribution contrcls on these items. Five copies of a press

market has achieved impressive results. It goes on to say that the
Japanese Cabinet has approved the Economic Stabilization Board plan to
continue and improve emergency control measures, adopted in December 1947,
through the present fiscal year. The new plan of the Board is claimed
tc tighten up weaknesses in enforcenent measures, new features including:
1. Improved fish preservation measures.
2.
Improved incentive goods distribution.
3.
Establishment of specific goels of 535 million ken
(2,z11,000 short tons) of fish and 620 million kan
(2,563,000 snort tons) of vegetables.
4. Estahlishment of model stores.

und vegetables has been and is unpopular, the following benefits are
claimed to have resulted:
1。
The proportion of fish sold through orficial channels
increased from thirty to seventy per cent and v getables
fron rive to seventy per cent.
2.
Total supplies of fish increased two times and vegetables
three times in major cities.
3. Prices were reduced about twenty-five to fifty per cent.

Comment:
We feel that the statement concerning the food situation in Japan
1s definitely encouraging and should have e favorable reaction among
Japanese. The fact that officials of this Headquarters have expressed
confidence that the ration of staple goods will be fully met during the
summer months and the ration of non-staple goods will be improved during
that time will, it is believed, help ease the anxieties of the Japanese
public. However, even when the standard ration is met the supply is
insufficient. It is obvious that people cannot sustain themselves
adequately when a considerable pert of the fixed ration is given in
substitutes. Thus the people are eugmenting their diet on black market
foods at fantstic prices to eke out a precerious livelihood.
The suggestion that after November 1 there may be an increase in
the ration of staple foods may stimulate the Japanese to work harder in
order to be able to afford an increasec ration. That the increase is
needed is indicated by the statement in this Headquarters 1948 Annuel
Report for Japan to Food end Agriculture Organization that since May 1946
surveys show that about twenty-five per cent of the population have had
symptoms related to nutritional deficiencies. (Copies of the report
were forwarded to the Departnent with this ?'ission's despatch No. 344
of June 1l, 1948.) In this connection, it is noted that Mr. Alber
states that the determining factor will be the success of the present
programs to increase Japanese lood production by ten per cent this year
and the successful collection of the pre-plantin guotas. The success
of last year's collection auurs well for this year's quotas being met.
We are more skeptical of : he possibility of a ten per cent increase
in Japanese fcod production this year. One reuson for scepticism is the
fact that sone or this ten per cent increase is expected to be derived
from an extension of erable acreage. As the Department is aware, however,
the Natural Resourees Section of this Heaiquerters thinks the Japanese
Government progran. for the inerease or food production by expansion of
arable acreage is overly optimistic, as can be judged by the following
quotation from the recent renort Outlook for Japanese Agriculture
prepured by that section (Preliminary Study No. 25 of May 6, 1948,
forwarded to the Depertment wit.out ov ring cespatch in pouch No. 2457
on Nay 11, 194):
"In smmary, (a) most of the desir ble land is already
in use, (b) most of the reelaimable land left is inf rior to
tre land now in cultivation, (e) the cost of reclamation on
some land is prohibitive, and (d) land which is not suitable
for reclamation has been ineluded in the program (of the
Japanese Government)."
Resnectfully yours,
l.eace
…. J. Sebald

FRESS CONFERENCE
STATELENT BY
H. F. ALBER, CaTEF, FRICE aD DISTRIBUTION DIVISION
ECONOMIC AND SC ENT IFIC SECTION, GHQ, SCAP
0.
FLOD SITUnTIUR II. JAPAN
TO1.YO
21 June 1948
The food outlook over the next four months is more promising than for
any summer since the surrender. The basic staple ration of 2.5 go and
existing supplemental rations will be wet in full until the harvest next
autumn. Local ration delays arising out of low stock levels, transportation
shortages and milling "bottle-necks" can be expected to continue. However,
such delays will be brief and the full ration will be made up quickly.
here will be no"skippeds ration days so people need not become alarmed
over the temporary co-ay wnich ay occur from time-to-time.
Rice will be imported into Japan this summer for the first time since
the war. Arrangements have recently been completed to ship Argentine corn
to Egypt in exchange for 40,000 tons (265,000 koku) of Egyptian rice. The
first ship load cf 3,500 tons is scheduled to arrive in lokohama late in
July. .orld supplies of rice are only abot half of pre-war levels and
deficit countries must secure allocations from the Rice Committee of the
International Emergency Food Connittee. The Rice Coluaittee, whic: consist
of representatives from most of the rice produc ing and importing countries
of the world, has felt that since tne Japanese war was largely responsible
for the decreased production of rice in asia, the victims of japan's ag-
gression should receive their rice requirements before any was sent to
Japan. The present rice imports have been ap,roved only as an cmergency
measure to prevent ration curtailment in the coming food deficit period
and do net establish any guarantee t. at Japan will receive furt .er rice
imperts.
To avoid rationing delays this year it has been necessary to issue
sugar in place of staple grains, and t: e substitution of sugar for rice will
continue through cctcber at the rate of about 4 days per month. after 1
hovember the distribution of sugar as a staple food substitute will decrease
shurply. It is recognized that sugar nas many shortcomings as a staple focd,
but in the absence of s fficient quantities of grain to reet the full ration
it has been necessary to eit er substitute sugar as an emergency easure or
to "skip" 3 to 4 ration days a month which could not be made u wit otier
foods.
A principle reason for local ration delays is the time-lag reguired to
process indigenous and imported grain caused by the limited flour milling
facilities available in Japan. To minimize this "bottle-neck" a major por-
tion of food imports over the next several months will consist of American
flour which will avoid the loss of time required fer further processing of
whole grain imports and will reduce the load on Japanese mills.
The food situation tis year shows a great improvement cver last year
when by the end of June, 15 to 25 "skipped" ration days were not wicommon.
As a result of this improvement, the Japanese peole are ass red a more
stable living and there is no cause for t.e major dependence on th black-
market that existed last year nor for the high food prices which developed
as a result of the food shortage.
However, it should be clearly understood that, meeting the full staple
ration depends on a successful collection of t e wheat, barley and potato
crops which are now being harvested and upon arrival of food imports on
schedule.

=== Page 441 === (confidence 0.97)
The outlook for non-staple foods is also good. The actual distribution
of miso will be increased gracually from the present rate of about 130 momme
per month to the full ration of ls0 morme (for central Japan) by November.
The actual distribution of shoyu will be increased about 20 % above present
levels during this same period a.c a regular ration of about 0.4 go of edible
oil per person er month wili be initiated in the near future. The fish and
vegetable rationin: program is continuing to operate effeciively and steps
are being taken to reduce hot veather spollage of these perishable foods.
The successful accomplishment of both staple and non-staple focd ration-
ing programs depends on unrelenting action against the blacimarket and re-
quires effective perfcrrance of all possible reasures to maximize Japan's
owm food supplies. Further.ore, these steps must be pushed as a condition
for te receipt of imported foods.
Since the end of the war, food imports to Japan have equalled approxi-
mately 25 & of the total staple fccd ration requirement. Cver the past 22
years a total of approximately 3,30c,000 tons of foods have been imported
at a cost of approximately 500,000,000. Since Deccmber 1947 approximately
950, 000 tons of imported foods ive been released and from July through
October of this year an adcitional 325,000 tons will be released in order to
neet the full staple ration. So far the United States has been meeting the
entire cost of tnese imports, ana the American people expoct that after
this year the Japanese ill assure an increasing siare of the cost of nec-
essary food imports. In orcer to mcet this obligation, japanese vorkprs
must maximize production for export and all consumors must expect to restrict
their consumption of goods so that larger supplies will be available fer sale
abroad.
There has been a great ceal of discussion in recent months about the
possibility of an increase in food rations. It should be clearly under-
stood that until at least 1 ove ber no staple ration increase will be
possible. Any later action waill depend on tre availability of increased
food supplies to neet the roptiresents of a hugher ration so at this time
no assurances can be given t'at the ration will be raised. An increased
food ration lovel fer Jay n tet ling 144o culories per day (as against the
present level of about 130 caleries) is being considered for effectuation
1 Novesber 1943. This ration voul consist of 2.7 go of stapls plus about
90 calorics per day of supar, ec bl cil and soya  reducts. substantial
dollar funds will be required to finance the im crts necessary to mcet this
ration and also food allocations must be sec red. ven if upproved, such
imports alone will net be sufficient to perrit ths rution increase. The
determining factor vill be t e suceces of present yrearams to i crease
Japanese food production by lo  tihns yeur nd successful collection of the
pre-planting quotas waich ave alreacy been established.
The Supreme Commander for the Allied Fowers is continting to tale all
possible aeastres to assure etsent al food sup. lies for Japan, but it is
the responsibility of the Japanese people to maximize food production and
to ration indigenous supplies efficiently and equitably.

SUBJECT: TokyO'ε 63 of January 29, 1949
Based on FEC-034/1 of June, 1946 requiring that measures
be taken on behalf of UN nationals and neutrals to insure
their health and welfare, SCAP has provided eince then
suyplementary food rations to cover minimum nutritional
requirements or non-enemy nationals and etateless persons.
These rations are supplied in one of two types at the pref-
erence of the recipient: the non-rice dietary to approximately
8,O00 persons (largely occidentals) and the rice dietary to
approximately 36,000 persons (largely Chinese, including
Formosan-Chinese).
Despite protests from various foreign national organiza-
tions 1n Japan, as well as the Chinese Mission, SCAP has
radually decreased thie supplementary ration in measure
FW 894.
as the reguiar ration for ali persons, including Japanese,
hae increascd. Today the total ration available to non-
Japanese is slightly greater than 1t was when the supple-
mentary ration system was establiehed.
SCAP is now coneidering the discontinuance of supple-
mentary food rations for foreign residents of Japan on the
grounds of the appreciable increase in Japanese focd rations,
5
the increa ed availability of non-rationed foods on the free
0
market, the present cost to the U.S. of more than 700,000
8
per year in providing additional imports necessitated by the
supplemented ratione, and the poasibility that the cost to
the U.S. may be substantially inoreased by the necessity of
1
providing the same supplementary rations to some 600,000
2
Korean recidente in Japan who may be able to establieh their
status as Korean nationals. Headquartere does not believe
that FEC policy decision requires the further continuance of 
supplementary food rations. Headquarters does recognize,
however, that to discontinue the occidental dietary would work
considerabie hariship on westerners in Japan who cannot import
weetern tyge foods from abroad, having no accese to foreign
exchange. It seems, however, that this situation could be
met ty the various foreien governments providing funds to the
U.S. by which adiitional foods could be imported for the bene-
fit of their nationals or to establish accounte in acceptable
currency with the SCAP eupcrvised Overeeas Supplies Store.
No

=== Page 459 === (confidence 0.96)
-2-
No action, required. If SCAP carries out the discontinuanoe
of these rations, however, strong protests may be expected both
to SCAP and to the FEC. I feel that thie 1s a matter that 1s
proyerly left to SCAp's determination and that unless our comments
are requested that we should avoid involvement in what appears
to be a rather complicated, technical issue.
Appraisal eheet 1s attached.

THE FOREIGN SERVICE

UNITED STATES OF AMERICA
United States Political Adviser
