Tokyo, January 29, 1949.
No. 63
RESTRICTED (FOR DEPARTMENT USE ONLY)
SUBJECT:
Supplementary Food Rations for Foreign Nationals
in Japan.
894.5018/1-2949
2
(
The Honoreble
 The 'Secretary of State,
Washington.
C3
Sirs
I have the honor to report that General Headquarters, Supremo
Commander for the Allied Powers, is considering the discontinuance
of supplementary food rations for foreign residents of Japan, and
that as a result of reductions already made in such supplementary
rations protests have been received from the Chinese Mission and
fror a committee representing the occidental residents of Kobe.
Considerations prompting the proposed discontinuance of
supplementary rations, which have been provided to foreign nationals
since the beginning of the occupation, are the increase in the
Japanese food ration, the increased availability of non-rationed
foods on the free market, the present cost to the United States of
more than $700, C00 per year in providing the additional imports
necessitated by the supplementary rations, and the possibility that
the cost to the United States may be substantially increased by the
necessity of providing the same supplementary rations to Koreans who
may be able to establish their status as Korean nationals.
Shortly after the occupation began the Japanese Government, under
oral instructions from this Headquarters, provided extra food supplies
to foreign residents. Food supplies were also distributed by the
International Relief Committee.
Following the adoption by the Far Eastern Commission on June 5,
1946, of a policy decision entitled "Aliens in Japan" which stated in
part that measures should be taken on behalf of United Nations
nationals and neutrals to insure their health and welfare, and in
order to standardize the supplementary ration already being made
available, the Japanese Government was directed, in SCAPIN lO69 of
July 19, 1946, to prepare for SCAP approval a plan for providing
supplementary food rations to cover the minimum nutritional require-
ments of non-enemy nationals and stateless persons. The plan as
approved
RESTRICTED

=== Page 488 === (confidence 0.98)
RESTRICTED
Tokyo's Despatch No. 63,
January 29, 1949.
-2-
approved by SCAPIN 1094 of July 30, 1946 provided a ration of
approximately l230 calories per day as a supplemont to the Japanese
food ration which was then ll24 calories per day for the normal
consumer. The supplementary ration was subsequently extended on
January 9, 1948 to include the national: of former enemy nations,
except Japan.
Supplementary food rations are supplied in one of two types,
at the preference of the recipient: the non-rice dietary to approx-
imately 8,000 persons, largely occidentals, and the rice dietary to
approximately 36,000 persons, largely Chinese including Formosan-
Chinese.
Although the Japanese ration has been increased from time to
time, and has provided 1550 to 1600 calories per day since November 1,
l948, no reductions were made in the supplementary rations until
December 1, 1948, when, in view of the disappearance of canned goods
from the Japanese import program and the increases in the Japanese
basic staple ration, the rice dietary and non-rice dietary supple-
mentary rations were revised and reduced to 846 calories and 827
calories per day, respectively, giving a total ration for non-
Japanese of approximately 2400 calories per day.
The monthly quantities of various items on the previous and
present supplementary rations (which are in addition to the Japanese
ration) are as follows:
"An Ration (Rice Dietary)

The portion of the Japanese ration which is supported by imports
to a level of 1440 calories per day includes only cereals, sugar,
fats and oils, miso and shoyu. In addition, fish and vegetables are
rationed, bringing the daily total of rationed foods to 1550 to 1600
calories for the normal Japanese consumer. Meat, eggs, butter, cheese,
fruits, certain seafoods, and all other foods are unrationed and, to
the extent that they are available, may be purchased on the free markot.
The inclusion of eggs, meat and butter on the supplementary ration
therefore merely makes these items regularly available to foreign
nationals at controlled prices.
The Chinese Mission, at the behest of the Chinese Overseas
RESTRICTED
Association

=== Page 489 === (confidence 0.98)
BESTRICTED
Tokyo's Dospatch No. 63,
January 29, 1949.
- 3 -
Association, an organization of Chinese nationals residont in Japan,
orally requested that the Japanese Government be instructed to
maintain the supplementary ration at the level established in 1946.
The Mission subsequently stated that there was no objection to the
reduction in the caloric value of the supplementary ration, in view
of the increased Japanese ration and the increased availability of
some non-rationed foods on the free market. but that the amount of
rice supplied on the supplementary rice dietary ration was inadequate
for the nutritional requirements of Chinese nationals. The Mission
further indicated that as a means of forcing the continuance of the
supplementary ration at the previous level the Chinese association
might encourage Chinese nationals to transfer from the rice to the
non-rice dietary, which it was felt (both by the Mission and by this
Headquarters) would disrupt the rationing procedure through the
difficulty of supplying large quantities of the items provided on
the non-rice dietary.
The Chinese Mission was informed that, inasmuch as the total rice
dietary ration now provides approxinately 24o0 calories per day, as
compared with 2354 calories per day at the time the supplementary
ration was established in l946, it was felt that the ration as cur
rently constituted adequately provides for the nutritional require-
ments of Chinese and other foreign nationals. The Mission was further
advised that the increase in the rice component of the basic Japanese
ration from 5.325 kilograms to 6.93 kilograms per month more than
offsets the l.4 kilogram reduction in the supplementary ration of rice.
There have been no instances of further difficulties in regard to the
supplementary ration for Chinese nationals.
11
In a letter dated January 5, 1949, which was sent to this Mission,
in its capacity as the Diplomatic Section of General Headquarters, for
transmittal to the Supreme Commander, the "International Committee of
Kobe", which describes itself as representing all occidental residents
of Kobe, requested an upward revision in the quantities of staple items
on the supplementary ration, the inclusion of certain additional items,
and a downward revision in prices of rationed goods. The committee
21
also sent letters of the same date to the Soviet Member, Allied Council
for Japan, and to the heads of all foreign missions in Japan (except
the Chinese, Philippine and Korean), enclosing a copy of its letter to
the Supreme Commander and giving a detailed explanation of the consider
3
ations which prompted the appeal. The French Mission, in a memorandum
of January 18, 1949, endorsed the committee's appeal, and it is probable
that some other foreign missions may do likewise.
The Economic and Scientific Section of this Headquarters, to
which the committee's letter has been referred, has informally
indicated to this Mission that there are numerous inaccurate state-
ments contained therein, such as that the increase of "4 lbs. in flour
cannot offset a decrease of 8 lbs. in potatoes". The Economic and
Scientific Section considers the food value of flour to be 3 to 4 times
that of an equal weight of potatoes.
This Mission considers that the reductions which have been made
in the supplementary rations can be justified adequately by showing
that the total ration is now slightly greater than it was when the
supplementary rations were established. However, in view of the
objections which have been made to the recent reduction in the supple-
mentary rations, strong protests may be expected to be made both to
SCAP and to the Far Eastern Commiesion in the event that the supple-
mentary rations are entirely discontinued.
It is the feeling of the Public Health and Welfare and the
RESTRICTED
Economic

=== Page 490 === (confidence 0.99)
RESTRICTED
Tokyo's Despatch Ho. 63,
January 29, 1949.
-4-
Economic and Scientific Sections of this Headquarters, as well as of
this Mission, that the FEC policy decision of June 5, 1946 does not
require the indefinite continuance of supplementary food rations for
foreign nationals, and that although such rations are made available
by the Japanese Government they place an unnecessary burden on the
United States, inasmuch as foods must be imported from the United
States to meet Japan's food deficit.
Apprehension is also felt in this Headquarters that an appre-
ciable number of the approximately 600,000 Koreans in Japan may
eventually qualify as foreign nationals and demand supplementary
rations on the same basis as other foreign nationals. In this
connection it should be mentioned that the statement in the FRC
policy decision of June 5, 1946 that the Japanese authorities should
"make adequate provision for the care, safety and welfare of
Formosan-Chinese and Koreans pending their repatriation" has never
been interpreted by this Headquarters as requiring supplementary
rations for Koreans, although the supplementary rations have been
made available to those Formosans who are registered as Chinese
nationals by the Chinese Mission.
The sections of this Headquarters concerned with the matter are
now considering the complete discontinuance of the supplementary
rations. It is planned to make adequate provision at the same time
for the needs of indigent foreign nationals on a higher level than
Japanese welfare cases, presumably by requiring the Japanese Ministry
of Welfare to provide sufficient relief aid to permit them to purchase
supplementary foods on the free market to equal the current ration
level of approximately 2400 calories per day.
4
There is enclosed a copy of an intra-Headquarters memorandum
(check sheet) from the Economic and Scientific Section dated January 13.
l949, showing that to provide the supplementary rations now costs the
United States approximately $728,510 per year, at a cost of approx-
imately $l6.55 per person, and that the cost to the United States would
be proportionately increased by providing the same supplementary ration
to Koreans in Japan who may be able to establish their status as
Korean nationals, and recommending that the supplementary rations be
discontinued effective April 1, 1949. The proposed action is now
under consideration by the Public Health and Welfare Section of tnis
Headquarters.
It is the feeling of the authorities concerned in this Headquarters
that the United States should not continue to provide indefinitely,
through the Japanese Government, the additional foodstuffs necessary
to afford the standard of living desired by foreign nationale in
Japan. It is recognized that some western-type foods are not available
in Japan, that such staple items as flour are not available on the
free market and are available on the Japanese ration in quantities
less than are ordinarily consumed by some occidentals, and that most
foreign nationals in Japan are unable to import such staples and
western-type foods from abroad because they have no access to foreign
currencies and cannot convert their yen holdings into foreign exchange.
It is the opinion of this Mission, however, that foreign govern-
ments which feel that their nationals in Japan cannot live adequately
under the Japanese rationing program might make arrangements either to
provide
RESTRICTED

=== Page 491 === (confidence 0.97)
RESTRICTED
Tokyo's Despatch No. 63,
January 29, 1949.
- 5 -
provide funds to the United States by which additional foods could
be imported for the benefit of their nationals, or to establish
accounts in acceptable currency with the SCAP-supervised Overseas
Supply Store by which the foreign nationals could secure such
additional foodstuffs as their governments feel are required.
Respectfully yours,
w.ixcaee.
W. J. Sebald

(COPY)
INTERNATIONAL COMMITTEE OF KOBE
30, Yamanoto-dori 2-chome
Ikuta-ku, Kobe
5th January 1949.
To the Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers,
Through the Chief of the Diplomatic Section, General Headquarters
Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers, Tokyo.
Sir,
As the Committee elected to represent all occidental
residonts of the Kobe area in matters affecting our common in-
terests, we take the liberty of subnitting a request for urgent
and considerate action to make the food ration for foreigners
more nearly adequate for our needs.
When in November l945 the Japanese authorities were
instructed to supply occidontal residents with certain supple-
mentary foodstuffs, in recognition of the different mode of life,
we sincerely appreciated it as a move in the right direction,
and hoped for a further gradual improvement, so much needed after
the hardsnips of the war-years.
To our regret, these rations were unfavourably "adjus-
ted" in July 1946; they permitted us, nevertheless, to continue
to ent. The new scnedule of Supplementary Food Rations recently
put into effect in accordance with a notification from the Vice-
Minister of Agriculture and Forestry to the Governor of Hyogo
Prefecture, dated 29th Novonser 1943, No. 23-S0-476, reduces our
rationed food, on whicn we have in the main to rely, to a level
which is decidedly insufficient.
We base our request upon three aspects:-
1. The overall decrease in the ration for foreign residents
since July 1946;
2.
The evident inferiority of present rations even when compared
with conditions in Great Britain, reputed to have the lowest
ration in occidental countries;
3.
The economic condition of the majority of foreign residents.
1. The overall decrease fron the rations stipulited in
November 1945 and valid until June 1946 1s reflected in the Co-
parative TAßLE A, attached. The increase of, say, 4 lbs. in flour
cannot offset a decrease of 8 lbs. in potatoes, while fats
(butter and oil) are reduced by well over half. An additional 15
eggs per month cannot in any way compare with 7 lbs. of assorted
canned goods, of which the largest proportion was prepared meat
and the balance (or bulk substitutes) included such items as
dried
RESTRICTED

=== Page 493 === (confidence 0.99)
RESTRICTED
Enclosure No. 1
to Tokyo's Despatch No. 63,
January 29, 1949
- 2 -
dried fruit, eggpowder and jams.
Furthermore, and repeatedly during the first period,
extra supplies like canned milk, vegetables, fruit and fruit
juices were issued. While the extra supplies ceased long ago,
the remaining 7 lbs. of canned goods were undoubtedly a mainstay.
2. TAßLE B tries to make a comparison with correspond-
ing British rations. The shortage of fats at once becomes evi-
dent, even if we receive slightly more butter. The total British
ration (including bacon and/or ham) i about 50x above ours.
Meat is approximately double the quantity allowed in Japan. Sugar
plus sweets amount to some 40. more, and to this may be added
an unlimited quantity of jams, officially unobtainable in Japan.
Flour and bread, potatoes, vegetables, fish and salt
are not rationed in Britain, while they are rationed bere. The
one item which we find free in Japan and rationed in Britain is
tea - which nevertheless is hardly obtainable in the open market.
The one case in which our rationing is superior to the British
is in the matter of about nine eggs a month.
In regard to dairy products we have considered the
"miso" ration as roughly of a nutritious equivalent to the British
cheese. The important difference lies in the fact that "miso" is
only an ingrediont, and hardly suited to the Occidental's taste,
and cheese is a useful food that helps the bread to become more
palatable. - While Britain issues a varying ration of milk (at
least 2.1/2 pints per week) our ration includes none at all, and
a fair quality is almost unobtainable in the market. Canned milk,
reserved for infants and invalids in theory, is issued only
against a doctor's certificate (which involves a fee), one tin
at a time, and after such a tortuous procedure that those needing
it prefer to resort to the black market, as less cumbersome and
hardly more expensive. The black market is well enough supplied
with both milk and cheese - and butter.
For butter we are charged the extraordinary price of
Yen 464.94 per pound, "inclusive of sales tax", while the same
butter, without great restrictions, can be purchased at the
Overseas Supply Stores at $ 1.-, or Yen 270.- per 1b.
The ceiling price (maruko) for eggs was fixed at
Yen 11.- per egg of 50 grams in May, 1948, and has not been amen-
ded. Yet the Hinistry of Agriculture and Forestry has permitted
to levy, in the case of rations for foreign residents, a "special
price" (reigei Kakaku) of Yen 18.87 per egg of equal weight.
"including sales tax". Evidently "ceiling" prices are not bind-
ing, even if, as we understand, the authorities are instructed
to deliver all rations at not more than ceiling prices approved
by General Headquarters. Eggs of fresher quality may be had in
the black market at not much more.
The Japanese ration does provide some "shoyu", as the
only seasoning apart from salt. No mention being made of any kind
of seasonings in the British rationing list, we must take it
that they are unrestricted; against which in Japan nothing what-
soever can be had in the legitimate market.
Two counts in the Britisn food scheme are of foremost
RESTRICTED
importance

=== Page 494 === (confidence 0.99)
RESTRICTED
Enclosure No. 1
to Tokyo's Despatch No. 63,
January 29, 1949
-3-
importancs in respect to total food supplies, and make them
incomparably better than ours:-
a) The 24 points per month issued per person, enabling him to
purchase certain restricted canned foods;
b) The possibility of eating standard meals at reasoneble fixed
prices in restaurants, without restriction and without pre-
judice to the rations.
No choice of additional rationed food is included in the Japanese
scheme. Restaurants are officially forbidden, and if a meal has
to be taken outside, one is forced to patronize a "black" ostab-
lishment, at incredible rates.
Nows from England confirms that numerous food items can
freely be bought at fair prices, there beiug almost no "black
market". It is well known that in Japan few items can be obtained
except in the black market. Those in the "open market" are quoted
almost as high, and being usually of inferior quality cause more
waste. This general condition creates a still wider disparity
between Great Britain and Japan in regard to "legitimate" food
supplies.
3. The financial condition of most foreign residents
in Japan has become such that they cannot purchase a sufficiency
of food in the available markets over and above the present
rationeú articles. Yet not only more bulk, but also a better
variety is imperative for even the most unpretentious nourishment
compatible with health. It is a well-known fact that calories
alone - which on the present basis seem entirely insufficient -
do not make for bodily wellbeing, and the psychological effects
of a monotonous and defective diet can be disastrous. Most resi-
dents are still trying to recover from the consequences of a war-
time diet.
We do not plead on behalf of the post-war arrivals who,
being the happy possessors of "hard currency", can purchase, or
receive from abroad, whatever they need. Neither do we plead on
behalf of that minority of pre-war residents who have managed to
acquire such wealth as shrugs its shoulders at the extravagances
of black markets. Yet investigation shows that of some 1450
residents registered in this district, by the end of November
last, only l73 persons among them, including post-war arrivals,
had access to the Overseas Supply Store, and of these, again,
many are not able to spend more than a few dollers per month,
the gift of some relative or friend abroad.
We are not posted in detail as to the situation of
foreign residents in other districts of Japan, but understand
that everywhere many of then are finding it difficult to meet
their daily expenditure. Reports from responsible men in Osaka,
Kyoto and the South are in line with this impression, and some
letters which appeared in English language papers of the North
would further confirm this. As regards the Kobe district, after
careful scrutiny we have arrived at the conclusion that not more
than one fourth of the foreign population is in a position to
supplement their rations in the manner required, without hard-
ships. Local salaries being completely out of line with costs,
they have
RESTRICTED

=== Page 495 === (confidence 0.99)
RESTRICTED
Enclosure No. 1
to Tokyo's Despatch No. 63,
January 29, 1949
-4-
they have to be supplemented by the sale or barter of personal
belongings. As nothing could be added to one's pre-war stock for
years, thet means a disposal of actual necessities in many cases.
Pre-war cash-assets have prectically disappeared through official
decrees and inflation.
Fifteen aged persons in the Kobe area are at present
receiving public relief, and we are afraid that more will have
to follow suit. The drastic cut in granted food, without a con-
siderable increase in the cash allowance, to permit "black" pur-
chases, will cause extreme distress in their ranks.
There can hardly be a doubt that the foreign residents
are averse to patronizing the black markets, theoretically
frowned upon by the authorities. But in order to cut them out,
the minimum of food required must be made available. In this
respect the needs of an Occidental are different from those of
an Oriental. At the same time the prices charged on certain items
which to us are of absolute necessity are such that, in view of
salaries paid, an official rationing becomes almost meaningless.
TABLE C, also attached, shows the increase in charges
made for rationed items during the past three years, which unfor
tunately could only include the 5 constant staple articles. Meat
had to be omitted because of the purely fictitious ceiling prices
up to August l948; fish showed enormous discrepancies in prices
quoted for small lots of various qualities placed at our dis-
posal. Canned goods and vegetables were too erratic, and the other
"foods" are a negligible entity. These five may be considered as
besic. The comparison of monthly expenditure for these, which by
no means constitute a living allowance, with fair average salaries
paid by Army offices to locel residents during the same period.
1s illuminating.
Japan has no free food market except for a few quite
secondary items. The official food allotment thus acquires para-
mount importance, and, based on the respective standard of living,
that of the foreigner has become less than adequate, ever since
October last, when canned goods were removed, although the offi-
cial notification reducing rations was issued as late as November
the 29th.
We extremely regret that, in view of the above consi-
dorations, we must place our appeal before the highest authority.
with our earnest r quest for an urgent upward revision of quan-
tities in regard to staple items, the inclusion of such articles
as milk, cheese, jans and condiments which make for a more pala-
table diet, and a corresponding downward revision in ration prices
where such do not depend on exchange rates.
In addition, instructions to the concerned for a more
regular and punctual distribution of allotments would be appre-
ciated, since delays mean deprivation for long periods, and pos-
sibly spoilage when several deferred lots are delivered at one
time.
Various interviews had by Committee members of our Food
Distribution Center with the Japanese authorities concerned have
been
RESTRICTED

=== Page 496 === (confidence 0.99)
RESTRICTED
Enclosure No. 1
to Tokyo's Despatch No. 63,
January 29, 1949
-5-
been completely negative, and complaints to pertaining Goneral
Headquarter Departments elicited sympathy but no action. As the
very existence of a valuable group of legitimate residents in this
country is affected, it has therefore been voted that our Committee
send copy of this appeal to the Heads of all Diplomatic Missions
at present in Tokyo, with explanatory comments. We must ask thom,
as a last resource, to take an active interest in the wellbeing
not only of their co-nationals but of the occidental community
in goneral. We have come to the conclusion, through bitter ex-
perience and too protracted patience, that the pre-war alien
without foreign currency, after undergoing undeserved hardships
during the war, is being gradually pushed into an abyss as an
undesirable entity. This, we know, is not in accordance with
the liberal world-policy of the Allied Powers.
In conclusion we wish to say that representativegof
our community will be ready to go to Tokyo at any desirod time
and on short notice, to discuss these and related matters.
We have the honour to be, Sir,
Yours respectfully
INTERNATIONAL COM ITTEE OF KOBE
U. A. Casal,
W. A. McIlwaine,
P. Bjoerstedt,
Chairman
Hon. Secretary
Hon. Treasurer

Explanatory Letter to
The Heads f te verious Diplomatic Hissions etc.,
FOKYO.
Gentlemen,
We beg leave to refer to the enclosed copy of our
to-day's Anpeal to the Supreme Commander of the Al ied Powers,
and must bespeak your support, on vehilf of your own nationals as
well as for those wis have no diplomatic representation in Japan
at the present time, for plaini. humanitarian reasons. We fear
that a point has been reacned where only intercession can help,
sirce apparently any move on the part of the Japanese authorities
to curtail what living facilities were left to the group concerned,
receives official sanction.
The second, acute decrease since the original SCAP
directives were issued soon after the end of the war, leaves our
regulated food supplies on a level entirely insufficient for
sustenance, it a tine when uncontrolled prices for foodstuffs have
attained heights where the ajority of occidental residents no
longer can afford adequate purcheses. The salaries, in general
based on what the Aray offices pay, have for lony been dispropor-
tionate to the devaluetion of the Yen - drastically reflected in the
cost of living - as vell us to the comparable earnings of Japanese
nationals. This in spite of the fact tivit the latters' corresponding
standard of living is acknowledged to be inferior, and that they have
means of obt inins supplies mravailable to the alien. The Japanese
rice ration, incidentally, their main basis, has gone up from a war-
time 2.1 "eo" per day per person to, firct 2.3 "go", then 2.5 "go".
and now .7 "o". Suniry "extras" pre apparently also distributed
on occasion, which ve no lon er receive.
A very recent augment tion in army salaries, which for
some time may not find its repercussion in priv te firms, is still
negligible, as the purehosing power remains way below the one which
a much soaller salary hed three years ago. It must not be over
looked that the reduction of the "nominal" exchange rate from 15 to
50 and then 270 per dollar vms fully followed by cost of living.
Aeminst a Yen devaluation of from 1 to 18 wit in the period, salaries
have now, at the very tail-end, been incrensed from 1 to 6.
The result is that people simply no longer can live from
their honest earnings. After conscientious investigatlon we have
come to the conclusion thit out of over l450 registered foreign
residents in our district only one guarter at the most, probably not
over

or not (unless naturally, Axis nationals, the remnant of whom have
apparently been absolved), were definitely pro-allies. The Japanese
knew it, and the foreigners were made to feel that they knew it.
When the Occupation Forces came, they were welcomed with
open arms. War-weary residents had nothing to give them except a
"home". We know how they appreciated this unselfish hospitality.
and from assurances received gather that it considerably contributed
to the morale of the young men in uniform. If a few homes made the
welcome an occasion for shady business, we can only say that there
must have been partners in the other camp. In the majority of cases
the reception was fendly, sincere, and without ulterior motive -
and offer enough at considerable cost to one's own comfort.
It was fully realized by the foreign residents that after
the war a return to a normal way of living could not be expected
within months. They were patient. But after almost three and a
half years, and with no peace on the horizon, they now see tlat while
more and more facilities are given to new arrivals, the unfortunate
war-timer's life becomes more and more difficult. The discrimination
is felt all the more keenly because many of these new arrivals are
but old co-residents and friends; fcrtunate in having escaped the
horrors and privations of actual warfare, but no whit the better of
those they left behind. Not only that, but sundry resident Japanese
are sudenly promoted to the full-fledged privileges of post-war
Americans, upon tre happy discovery of an accidental birth in USA...
In various ways, insult is added to injury.
With a food situetion which is now declared to be mark-
edly better tian it was three years ago, old resideuts are "entitled"
to considerably fewer calories, less bulk, less variety in their
already unbalanced diet. At the same time the black market for
critical items - which in view of the initial insufficiency of
"rations" had alweys to be patronized - has not ouly become far more
expensive but has gone more underground, is harder to get at for the
suspicioned alien.
We do not argue in favour of a wider black market.
Quite on the contrary, we dislike the very idea of it having to be
resorted to, and believe that with a better will and sincerity much
more could be done to abolish it. In the meantime the few items
that one can vuy in the open market - at prices not much lower than
"black" but of inferior quality - do not provide either the calories,
bulk or variety of food such as an Occidental ofeven the most frugal
habits needs for his health. Especially after several years of
preceeding debilitation due to undernourislment.
The generosity of the American public, the American
Government and Army, towards defeated and other necessitous nations
is too well known to be doubted by us. We can only surmise that
there must be a misrepresentation or exaggerated technicality in
reard to this handful of aliens, a stumbling-block which it should
not be impossible to remove, though we do not seem to succeed ourselves.
Unfortunately the foreign residents have too long trusted in  gradual
amelioration of their situation. Instead of which they are now
notified

notified of a further and serious agravation.
Since it is the declared intention of the United States
to improve the standard of living of oriental peoples, it seems
entirely contradictory and illogical that Occidentals used to a
very igh standard, and who have so far quite successfully taught
the Japanese in the matter of better alimentation, should not only
be neglected but relegated to a level imccmparably inferior to what
the Japanese have been accustomed to for ages. Yet while appeals
made in various quarters by appointed representatives of this
community usually elicted expressions of sympathy, nothing further
ever happened to relieve the discouraging circumstances, which on the
contrary are allowed to go from bad to worse. Sympathy alone does
not nourish, and when it is recalled tht in the whole of Japan there
are not more than a few thousand occidental residents (less than
1500 in this major district of Kobe) more favourable arrangements
do not seem beyond possibility.
If we analyze the system by which the Japanese
Government pretends to abolish the black market of food (as well as
other items), we come to the conclusion that it is by pegging the
official prices to a level just below (in a few instances actually
above!) the current "black" ones, which, now, represent the
approximate maximum which the Japanese people can stand.
Salaries paid by private firus to foreign resident
employees are usually based on those paid by the Army. In the
Army such salaries, even after the recent increase are at a rate
less than 20 per cent of the sulary paid to Department Army Civilians.
In order to bring salaries of foreign resident employees into line
with cost of living and the earnings of the Japanese (on an average
higher than those of a foreigner) a large and immediate increase
would have to be made.
Numerous others, bowever, could not benefit from even
such an adjustment. It would, in cenerel, be much preferable to
receive a sufficiency of fit foodstuffs, in adequate quantity and
variety, at more moderate prices.
The group of destitutes at present at the public charge
in the meautime will have to be compensated without delay for any
reduction in the official retions. The present arrangement, under
SCAP directive, is that they be iven their complete rations free,
plus a scanty monetary allowance. aving to purchase a greater
amount of food in the arkets, the monetary allowance (meant to cover
rent, light, fuel, clothin and incidentals) will no longer be
sufficient to prevent starvation. This group, we repeat, is bound
to increase. The charitable LARa institution exclusively supplies
the Jananese needy.
Permit us a few further comments on the food-rations
which non-Army civilians now receive.
Certain food-items should be included in the rations as
teing essential to the Occidentals, although not important to the
Japanese

Incidentally, the quantity allotted always contains a remarkable

amount.
Fresh meat has been put back on the quota for 1945-6
300 momme per month (about 1.1/3 ounce per day), but three years
ago we received, in addition, 7 lbs. of canned goods, most of which
was meat of some condensed kind. The present price of meat, in
spite of a speciel reduction from ceiling price, is very high for
many foreigners. It is not its cost alone which counts: the cooking
involves further expenditure for fuel and ingredients.
With electricity almost unavailable for cooking, gas
reduced to a minimun and poor in quality, coal not forthcoming
even to a small quantity promised, wasteful because of dust, and
unattainable to the average purse, a standard distribution of char-
coal at reasonable price should be granted. An average irregular
supply of half a "oale", say some 20 lls., per household per month
(1948), of inferior and dusty quality, is quite insufficient.
It should not be overlooked that the foreign resident
has no opportunity to eat a meel or even a sandwich outside of his
house, except at fantastic prices in "illegal" restaurants. Army
employees are slightly better off, but few can take more than the
lunch, partly for family reasons, partly because there is no place
where they can spend the intervening time. There have been un-
pleasant remarks on the "greediness" of employees takins lunch only.
when, as a matter of fact, this one meal may e almost all their
daily food. The charges are monthly ones based on three daily meals.
It would considerably alleviate the lot of many if they
were permitted to buy in the Overseas Supply Stores by purchasing a
fixed amount of Dollars per month through an official agency, at the
Army exchange rate, and provided they were not otherwise in receipt
of "acceptable currency". A recommendation to this effect was made
in a letter dated 14th October l948, addressed to the Commanding
General, I Corps, by Lt. Col. George L. Atwood, Commanding Hyogo
Military Government Team, to whom the sitwation was fully and frankly
explained, and who evidently realized the great difficulties of the
aliens in Japan. So far nothing furt er has been heard. A
possibility of varying one's meals with the help of certain condiments
- which were always imported and always in use oy Occidentals - seems
to become an imperative necessity. Not even tomato-sauce is
available, and almost nothing to make a sandwich.
Reluctantly, and only because further procrastination
might threaten the very existence of a group of occidental people,
have we resclved to unadornedly place the circumstances before the
appointed representatives of our various governments, on behalf of
all, and to request their good offices in arriving at some fair and
workable solution. We believe cur strong claim for more consideration
of the old resident justified - he, too, was and is an ambassador of
occidental culture and of a higher plane of living. It does not seem
right that allied, neutral and stateless Occidentals whould suffer
under comparatively greater handicaps than the Japanese themselves, in
whose war, after all, they were involved against their will. It would
appear better justice if the Japanese authorities, who indulged in
their antipathies during the war, were now made to offer redress by a
markedly

Tokyo, 1e January 18, 1949.
MEMORANDUM
To
SCAP, DIPLOMATIC SECTION
Subject : Status of Foreign Nationals residing in Japan.
The French Mission in Japan presents its compliments to
the Diplomatic Section of SCaP and, referring to the letter from
the International Committee if Kobe dated January 5, 1949, also
sent to the Diplomatic Section, has the honor to makeknown that
the reasons which have dictated this document seem to be fully
justified. The French Mission thinks that, for considerations
of impartiality and humanity, the facilities granted to Foreign
nationals living in Japan - facilities that a great number cannot
afford on account of local conditions - should be distributed
in a better way.
Therefore, the French Mission has the honor to inform that
it would approve any step which could bring e fair solution to
this problem.

1. This Check Sheet formalizes conversations between ESS,
PH&W, G-1, Diplomatic Section, and the Religious and Cultural
Division of CIE with respect to the elimination of the supplemen-
tary ration to foreign nationals because of the improved food
conditions in Japan, and the imminent establishment a a Korean
Embassy and subsequent eligibility of approximately 600,000
Koreans for the Foreign Nationals supplementary ration (under
SCAPIV 1069, dated 19 July 1946).
2. The historical background for the establishment of a
supplementary ration to Foreign Nationals has been thoroughly
checked. A JCS conmunication or 13' June 1946 to the Supreme
Commander or the Allied Powers (JCS/S1) contains a directive
relative to the treatment of all aliens in Japan. This directive,
prepared by the U. S. Department of the State implements the
policy adopted by the FEC on 5 June 1946. The wording of several
paragraphs implies that the responsibility of the Japanese govern-
ment for the welfare of aliens is of a temporary nature, covering
the period during which they are preparing for voluntary departure
to their countries of origin. As illustration of the implied
temporary natire of this responsibility, the following paragraphs
from JCS/51 are quoted:
"a. Aliens shall be allowed, if they so desire, to leave
Japan as early as possible, except such persons who are held as
war criminals or for security reasons:
"b. Subject to your general control, the Japanese
authorities should be held responsible for the care, safety, and
welfare of such persons and for their transportat on to ports of
embarkation."
Again under "d" of JCS/5l: - "Responsibilities of the Japanese
Authorities: Measures should be taken on behalf of United Nations
nationals and neutrals to insure the health and welfare and trans-
portation to a port from wiich they may embark for transoceanic
destinations." In no FEC or JCS communication is it directly
stated or implied that the responsibility of the Japanese Govern-
ment to provide special privileges and treatment for foreign
nationals residing in Japan shell be of a permanent nature.

=== Page 510 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No. 4 to

- 2 -
BASIC: Check Note No. 1 from ESS to G-1, Thru PH&W, DS, subj:
"Elimination or Supplementary Ration for Foreign Nationals
3. To insure the health and welfare of aliens in Japan at a
period when the food shortage was critical and the mechanism of
distribution highly disorganized, SCAPIN l069 was issued in July
1946 by PH&W formalizing the responsibility of the Japanese Govern-
ment to "cover the minimum nutritional requirements" at regular
poriode in a standardized manner. The contents of the supplementary
ration was based on the physiological needs of foreigners as well
as on availabilities. In July of l946, the total rationed foods,
both staple and non-staple, amounted to ll24 calories per capita
per day for the normal consumer. Not infrequently even this low
ration level could not be maintained. The free market offered
little and many Japanese managed to survive only by frequent trips
to the country to forage.
4. Two and a half years have now elepsed. Foreign Nationals
now remaining in Japan, with few exceptions, are here because they
choose to make Japan their home. The majority of foreign nationals
have reestablished themselves economically to a greater or lesser
degree. The food situation,although still requiring strict controls
of basic items and significant imports of cereals, pulses, sugar,
and fats and oils, has improved to a marked degree. The basic ration
of cereals, sugar, fats and oils, miso and shoyu now amounts to
l440 calories a day for the normal consumer. During the past year,
no ration days have been skipped. With rationed fish and vegetables
included, the average daily caloric value of all rationed foods
included in the Japanese basic ration is l550-1600 calories per day.
In addition, while admittealy at higher prices, the free market is
again functioning, and now offers a wide selection that includes
meat, eggs, butter, shellfish, most high grade fish, peanuts and
fruit. Although the additional ration of flour and rice provided
by the supplementary ration is not available on the free market,
the 2.7 "go" ration in effect since November 1948 plus the free
market foods now available can provide ample calories to satisfy
the minimum requirements of foreign nationals wiih a better and
more balanced diet than the present supplementary ration with its
high carbohydrate component.
5. It is the policy of the U. S. to finance from appropriated
funds the imported food necessary to support a l440 caloric level.
Thus airectly, or indirectly, additional ration requirement must be
considered an adaitionel charge on the U. S. Treasury. At present
there are approximately 36,O00 Foreign Nationals (principally
Formosans and Chinese) receiving the rice dietary supplementary
ration, and approximately 80o0 foreign nationals receiving a non-
rice dietary supplementary ration of whom 579 are stateless. The
table below shows the annual cost per capita for the supplementary
rice dietary - the present total cost of the supplementary ration
program in terms of imported foods (assuming the cost of the rice
and non-rice dietary to be approximately the same), and the
aduitional cost in terms of imported food for the supplementary
ration ror 600,000 Korean.

BASIC: Check Note No. 1 from ESS to G-1, Thru PH&W, DS, subj:
Elimination of Supplementary Ration for Foreign Nationals
6. It is concluded that:
a. Present circumstances with respect to the repatriation
of foreign nationals and the general food condition in Japan point
to the need for a revision of the present policy vis-a-vis the
supplementary ration for foreign nationals.
b. The continuation of the supplementary ration will
entail additional rationing obligations valued at about 10,000,000.
7. It is recom ended that:
a. As of 1 April l949, all sup ,lementary rations for
foreign nationals shall be eliminated.
b. That indigent foreign nationals on whom the elimina-
tion of the supplementary ration inflicts a demonstrable hardship
shall, as indigents continue to be the responsibility of the
Japanese Government and will be supplied with the quantities and
kind of free market foods considered necessary for their minimum
requirements at the expense of the Japanese Government.
